[trialAccount t18290409-280]
959. WILLIAM HILLMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March. 3 jugs, value 2s. 6d.; 12 pap boats, value 3s.; 4 busts, value 5s.; 65 egg-cups, value 1ls.; 58 cups, value 6s. 6d.; 54 saucers, value 5s. 6d.; 29 basons, value 7s.: 1 milk-pot, value 3d.; 7 glass cruets, value 14s.; 144 toys, value 2s.; 148 plates, value 18s. 6d.; 54 saltcellars, value 8s.; 12 coffee-cups, value 3s. 6d., and 1 china dog, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Mansfield and another, his masters; and EDWARD PAYSON was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, he well knowing them to have been stolen.
JOHN MANSFIELD. I manage the business of the prosecutors, who are earthenware manufacturer s, and live in Barbican. Hillman was in their employ for three or four years, as a kind of porter - I was going along Smithfield on the 26th of March, and overtook Hargrave with a basket of goods on his head; he did not give me any information, but I sent a person to Payson's with my young man, who is not here; he brought a pattern of some goods; I went to Payson's lodging on the 2d of April, in Three Tun-court, Redcross-street, and found him there - I told him I had accused my young man, Hillman, of robbing me, and he had confessed it, and said that Payson had received the goods and paid 6d. per dozen for them; he said he had not, but that he had received the goods for gown pieces, which Hillman had had of him, and he had given him some money besides; the price of the goods were two-thirds less than they could be purchased for on his own account - I saw the two prisoners afterwards together; Payson asked Hillman whether what I had said was true - Hillman said it was, about his paying 6d. a dozen for them; Payson denied having paid any money for them, except two or three shillings - there was property found in both their lodgings, cups, saucers, and a great many other things; the greater part was found in Payson's lodgings.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. What did you find at Hillman's? A. The officer has it; I had a communication with Hillman before I saw Payson, but not before I sent to him - I did tell Hillman that if he would confess the extent to which he had been robbing us, I would not prosecute him; and he said, what Hargrave had was all he had robbed us of.
GEORGE HARGRAVE. I deal in earthenware, and live in Sandy-street, Bishopsgate. I have seen the prisoners, but not in company - I dealt with Payson once only, which was on the 26th of March; I bought twelve dozen of plates, five dozen of egg-cups, and four dozen and half of salts; I paid 1s. 3d. a dozen for the plates - it was no very great bargain - I could buy them at Mr. Mansfield's for 1s. 4d.; I have the articles now at home - I have sold about a dozen egg-cups; Mr. Mansfield saw me going along, and told me they were his; Payson told me he had bought them at Mr. Bullen's sale.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. Where does Mr. Mansfield live? A. In Barbican; I bought them between eleven and twelve o'clock, and he met me about one - I had seen Payson the day before in Petticoat-lane, where I stand every afternoon to sell goods; he asked me if I would buy some blue muffin-plates, and told me he lived at No. 3, Three Tun-court, Redcross-street - I offered him a 5l. note; there was some difficulty about changing it, and I went to Mr. Mansfield for change as I deal there - I told him I should go there; he made no objection - I returned in about two hours, and he was still there.
JOSEPH ORTON. I am a constable. I was sent for toMr. Mansfield, and took Payson to the computer - I found on him 1l. 0s. 9d., and the key of his door; I went to his lodgings, and found the greater part of this property concealed under a bedstead, which goes back with folding doors, from the top to the bottom - here are three pairs of images, three china jugs, one blue mug, twelve blue papboats, four busts, one blue butter-pan, seventy-two eggcups, twenty-one blue basons, and a great variety of other things which Mr. Mansfield identified; Payson said he would bring witnesses to prove that he bought part of it of Mr. Mansfield - the next morning I asked him if he could bring those witnesses; he said,
"I never told you so" - I went with Howard to take Hillman, and on him was found a key which opens Payson's door.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. Did Payson tell you where he lodged? A. Yes; I did not know him before - it is a small room; the things were mostly under a bedstead - there were a number of basons in a closet, some earthenware in other parts of the room, and images on the mantel-piece; the other articles were on a sofa under a coat.
ROBERT HOWARD. I am a constable. I went to Mr. Mansfield's and then to Hillman's lodging, where I got twelve coffee cups, five cruets, and one china dog - I found this key on him, which opens Payson's lodging.
The prosecutors not being able to identify the property, the prisoners were ACQUITTED.
[trialAccount t18290409-281]
959. JAMES HILL was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 2 tea-pots, value 8s. 6d.; 1 jug, value 1s. 3d.; 1 pair of snuffers, value 4s. 6d.; 1 japan tray, value 2s.; 4 brushes, value 3s. 6d.; 2 cushions, value 1s. 2d.; 2 phosphorus boxes, value 6d.; 5 combs, value 3s.; 2 other combs, value 2s.; 1 gold ring, value 4s., and 72 pencils, value 2s. 3d., the goods of John Sewell and another, his masters.
JOHN SEWELL, JUN. I am in partnership with John Sewell, of Fore-street; we are jewellers and hardwaremen - the prisoner was our porter; we suspected him, and spoke to the officer, who was in waiting, and took him on the 24th of March - he had been with us five or six years.
JOHN LACEY HAWKINS. I am a marshalman. I watched the premises on the 24th of March - I took the prisoner on the opposite side of the way, and took him into his master's house; I saw him put his hat on a chair on his left hand - I said he should not put any thing down; I stopped down, and when I got up I saw this packet of pencils in the chair on his left hand - I did not see it come out of his hat; there were no persons present but Mr. Sewell and the prisoner - I searched the prisoner's house in Castle-street, Finsbury. to which he directed me, where I found these other articles; here is a metal tea-pot, a tea-tray, combs, brushes, phosphorus-boxes, and other things, and a gold ring.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. Have they been claimed? A. Yes, by Mr. Sewell; I believe there were two or three plated spoons which Mr. Sewell said he could not swear to, and I did not take them - I took the prisoner, into the back parlour at his master's, Mr. Sewell went up with us, but he got into the room before me; the room had been empty - I cannot tell how the parcel came on the chair.
MR. SEWELL re-examined. Q. What does the parcel contain? A. Pencils; there is a mark on the paper; most of the other things had a private mark on them; I believe them to be ours:; the prisoner sent two letters the next day - one to my mother, and one to myself; I have seen him write, and believe they are his writing - this is the one to my mother - ( reads.)
HONOURED MADAM, - On account of my unfortunate wife and children, I hope you will not press this charge against me; particularly when I assure you I intended to replace the articles, or to have given you the value for them. I hope you will take my unfortunate case into your consideration, and treat me with that lenity I know your feeling heart would wish; and, as in duty bound, I shall ever pray, Your's, J. HILL.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. Was there a relative of your's who lodged in the prisoner's house? A. Not that I know of; I have been in the country a good deal lately; I did not know till the other day that my mother used to visit a person at the prisoner's.
JAMES SEWELL. I saw the prisoner take these pencils out of his hat, and put them on the chair.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you out when the prisoner was laid wait for? A. No, I was in the warehouse - I went up stairs with them; I went in first, with a light, and the prisoner went in next; I took the parcel out of the chair, and examined it - it has our mark on it; I did not know of any relation lodging at the prisoner's.
GUILTY. Aged 29.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18290409-282]
960 THOMAS EEDLE was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 wooden box, value 4d.; and 400 oranges, value 19s., the goods of John Knill.
JOHN KNILL. I am a fruit broker, and live in Pudding-lane. A box, containing four or five hundred oranges, was on its way from a ship, at Cox's quay, to the warehouse - the prisoner was employed by my brother as a porter, to carry it - it has not been seen since.
HENRY KNILL. I am the prosecutor's brother; I am a fruit cooper, and work the fruit on board the ships - the prisoner worked regularly as a porter; I had employed him many times before and I employed him on the 13th of March to carry these oranges from the vessel I was on board of, at Cox's quay, to the seed warehouse, which is between Cox's quay and Thames-street; he was to deliver it - to one of the warehouse men: I saw him go off with the box.
WILLIAM PARRY. I am a porter - I followed the prisoner with another box of oranges, on the 13th of March - I saw him go on beyond the seed warehouse, which is about one hundred yards from the quay - I hailed him - he did not stop, but walked on, and turned the gateway, and I saw no more of him.
RICHARD BUTTER. I am a porter. I saw the prisoner pitch a box of oranges on the block in King's Headcourt; I asked him where he was going - he took no notice of me - that was beyond the warehouse - I then passed on.
THOMAS BRIAN. I am a porter. I was going into the King's Head public-house, to light my pipe - I met Tom Shields, who asked me to take a drop of beer; I came out, and he said, this box was to go to No. 112, Newgate-street, and if I would take it he would give me 1s.; I took it - the prisoner said nothing to me about it.
Q. Did you see the prisoner afterwards? A. Why, I did see him - but Shields is the man who employed me; the prisoner never mentioned a sentence to me concerning the box whatever; Shields was the man who employed me and paid me.
COURT. Q. Do you mean you never talked to the prisoner about it afterwards? A. I did not speak a word to him about it nor he to me; I met him on the way.
Q. Did you nod to each other, or say "How do you do?" A. It might be the case, for all that I know.
Q. Did not he mention about the oranges or the box? A. No; not who employed me - nor any thing about it: I did meet him on my journey from Pudding-lane to Newgate-street - I had the box on my knot; I cannot tell which side of the way he was; I cannot tell which way he was going - I knew where I was going myself; I rather think he might be about Cheapside.
Q. Did you pitch at all? A. Why I got rather tired, and the prisoner took it on his head, as I was rather tired - he carried it from Cheapside to where I was ordered to take it - to assist me, I rather thought, from where I pitched it.
Q. What made you tell me that you did not know that you had spoken to each other? A. I did not mention that.
Q. Do you know you are bound by your oath to speak the whole truth, as well as nothing but the truth - then the prisoner, when you pitched, and were heavy loaded and fatigued, carried it to this place, did he? A. Yes, he did take it on his knot, in Cheapside, to where I had been ordered to take it, to No. 112, Newgate-street.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is Shields here? A. I understand not; he employed me to carry this box, and said it belonged to a countryman, who was rather drunk, and could not carry it.
Q. Was the prisoner near Shields at that time? A. No; Shields was the person who said he would pay me, and did pay me - it is customary for us porters to give one another a lift, all in good-nature; I cannot say what it contained; I dare say oranges - I did not look into it -Shields agreed to give me one shilling, and he gave me half a crown.
Q. The prisoner never gave you a farthing? A. No; he has often given me a lift when I have been tired - I have known him three years; he never did assist me before, nor I him; I only meant to say that us porters are in the habit of doing it - Shields paid me when I came back; we did not speak a word to one another going back; it was not Shields who helped me; I never saw him till I got back and he paid me.
Q. Did the prisoner, directly or indirectly, employ you to carry that box? A. He never did: I was examined before the Lord Mayor; I told there every word that I have to-night - I said Shields agreed for 1s., and gave me 2s. 6d., and that the prisoner gave a lift on the road.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 31.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18290409-283]
961. THOMAS RICKARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 2 ozs. of tea, value 3d., the goods of the United Company of Merchants trading to the East Indies .
MESSRS. BOLLAND and LAW conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM KEYS. I am in the employ of the East India Company. On the 7th of April the prisoner was employed in a room on the ground-floor, at their warehouse in Cutler-street; he was mending one of the chests; I saw him take some tea out, and put it into his left-hand pocket; I informed Mr. French, the assistant elder - this was between eight and nine o'clock in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Had you, the minute before, had any conversation with him? A. Yes; I had asked him for snuff - he did not put his hand in his pocket, take out some tea, and say, "This is tea which I forgot to deliver where I was directed;" nor say, "My God ! I have the tea in my possession which I ought to have left at home;" he was taken into custody in about ten minutes - he was in custody before nine o'clock.
WILLIAM FRENCH. I am assistant elder to the East India Company. Keys gave me information, and I called the prisoner; I took hold of him; he wanted to get his hand from me to get to his pocket - I took him into the counting-house, and there put my hand into his left-hand pocket, which was half a yard long, and had been made for the purpose; I took out this tea, and put it on a sheet of paper; here are from two to three ounces of it - I gave him in charge, and went to examine the chest he had been working at; I found the lead had been cut at the side, and there was an aperture made that the hand could go in, and take the tea out; the tea found in his pocket exactly corresponded with that in the chest.
Cross-examined. Q. How long has he been in the Company's service? A. Sixteen or seventeen years.
JAMES TOMKINS. I am in the employ of the East India Company. On the 7th of April I took the prisoner into custody, at the request of French; he rather tried to get his hand from Mr. French.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18290409-284]
962. ELLEN FLINN was indicted for a misdemeanor. MESSERS. BOLLAND and LAW conducted the prosecution.
JOHN MARTIN. I am servant to Mr. Lockley a pastry-cook, of Newgate-street, On the 31st of March, about halfpast ten o'clock at night, the prisoner came and asked for a penny cake, she gave me a shilling, and I gave her 11d. change; I afterwards saw Lee, the watchman, and showed the shilling to him - he gave it me again, and I put it into a drawer in which there was only a sixpence besides; the prisoner came again in about a quarter of an hour; she then asked for a penny cake, and gave me another shilling, which looked black on the head - I detained her, and called in the watchman; I called my master, and delivered the two shillings to him; he marked them in my presence, and they were delivered to the watchman.
WILLIAM LEE. I am a watchman. I saw the prisoner in the shop - when she came out, I went in and saw the shilling, which Martin showed me; the prisoner went there a second time - I was called in and took her; I found 7 3/4d. in copper on her; these are the two shillings which were delivered to me.
WILLIAM JONES. I am a constable. The prisoner wasbrought to the watch-house - I searched her, and found 7 1/2d. in copper on her; the two shillings were delivered to me - I returned them to Lee.
JOHN FIELD. I am inspector of counterfeit coin; these shillings are both bad - they are cast in the same mould, and are made of what is called white metal.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Confined Six Months, and to find Sureties.
[trialAccount t18290409-285]
OLD COURT.
SEVENTH DAY. THURSDAY, APRIL 16.
Second Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
968. SARAH EVANS was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charles Rushbrook, on the 14th of March, and stealing 4 coats, value 10l.; 5 boys' dresses, value 8l.; 2 waistcoats, value 20s.; 2 pairs of trousers, value 2l.; 3 yards of cloth, value 50s., and 1 pair of drawers, value 16s., his property.
CHARLES RUSHBROOK. I live in Leather-lane, at the corner of Baldwin's-gardens, and am a tailor. I went to bed about eleven o'clock at night on the 13th of March, and got up about a quarter-past six in the morning; I had left my house quite secure, but found a piece cut out of one of the shop shutters, round the corner in Baldwin's-gardens - it had been cut by an orger, and I found the piece inside that had been cut out; they then broke one of the squares of glass, and brought the property through the hole - they must have put a hand in to have got the property; I missed property worth 25l. or 30l. - I know nothing of the prisoner.
ROBERT TYRRELL. I am a Bow-street officer. On the 14th of March, about half past twelve, or one o'clock in the day, I saw the prisoner going down Sun-street, with this bag in a bundle-handkerchief; she was threequarters of a mile from the prosecutor's; two men were ten or fourteen yards behind her, and then went in front of her; I cannot say they were in her company - she went in a direction for Petticoat-lane - the two men then got in front of her; I knew one of them, which excited my suspicion, and in a street leading to Petticoat-lane I went up to the prisoner, and asked what she had got there; she said she did not know - I asked her where she had brought it from; she said she had met a man in Smithfield, who asked her to carry the bundle, and he would give her a shilling - I asked her where she was to carry it to; she said she did not know, but the man told her to follow him - the two men had then got in front of her, as far as they were before from her; I asked her if either of the men walking in front was the man who gave it her - after some hesitation she said it was the stoutest, and at that time one of the men looked round, and both walked fast; they turned the corner, and were out of sight in a minute - I took her to the office, with the bag, which contained this property.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18290409-286]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
964. PATRICK O'DONNELL and PETER DALEY were indicted for feloniously assaulting Elizabeth Coombs, on the King's highway, on the 30th of March, at St. Luke, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, 1 bonnet, value 3s.; 1 shawl value 1s.; 1 handkerchief, value 3d.; 1 pair of patterns, value 6d., and one sixpence, her property.
ELIZABETH COOMBS. I am single, and live in May-place, Stamford-street, Blackfrairs-road. On Sunday night, or Monday morning, the 30th of March, between one and two o'clock, I was going home, and O'Donnell spoke to me, at the end of Fleet-street; he asked me where I was going - I never saw him before; I am certain he is the man; I told him I was going home; he said he would give me half-a-crown if I would take a walk along with him - I said I knew no place on this side the water, and he said he did; I turned, and went with him; he took me to Lion and Lamb-court, Golden-lane - I was never there before; we did not go into a house; the patrol came up, and asked if I lived there; and he asked O'Donnell if he lived there, and he said Yes; the patrol told me I had better go home, it was quite time I was at home, and the man likewise - I turned from the prisoner, and asked the patrol to direct me to Blackfriars-bridge - he took me a little way below the brewhouse, and told me to go across Smithfield, and that was the way to the bridge; I had scarcely left the patrol a minute before O'Donnell came after me, tapped me on the shoulder, and told me he would give me another shilling if I would go back with him - I refused, and said I was going home; but I afterwards turned, and went back with him - when I came back to Lion and Lamb-court he showed me the house, No. 8, and told me that was the house I was going to; but instead of taking me into the house, he put one hand on my shoulder, and the other under my arm, and violently tried to push me into a place opposite, and I felt two more men trying to pull me in likewise; I screamed out for the watchman -O'Donnell then called out for Burke, and told him to stop the b-y b-chs throat; O'Donnell directly up with his fist, and struck me on the side of my head, and knocked me down in the court; I felt them pulling my things off as I lay in the court; I cannot say which of them it was - they pulled off my bonnet, shawl, handkerchief, and pattens; O'Donnell directly put one hand across my mouth and nose, and the other on my throat, and the others were pulling me, one by the legs, and the other by the shoulder - I heard some footsteps coming up the court: and directly they heard the footsteps, they left me, and ran into the house, No 8, opposite.
Q. Are you sure of O'Donnell doing what you have mentioned? - A. Yes; there were two besides him - I could not swear to either of them; I cannot say which of them pulled my things off: they were holding me on the ground while my things were being pulled off - O'Donnell held me on the ground while my things were pulled off; they were all three nearly together; while I was on the ground, O'Donnell struck me several times on my head, while I was being thrown down, and while I was down, and he put his hand over my mouth and nose - the other two men seemed to be in the place opposite when they began to pull me: I had a shilling and sixpence tied up in the corner of the handkerchief, which they took from me- I should not know either of the other two men: they all went into No. 8; the watchman came up - I was then on my hands and knees, trying toget up; I told him what had happened; he looked about for my things, but nothing was to be found; I also looked about for them - the watchman had a lantern, and there was a gas-light in the middle of the court; the watchman then went into No. 8, and I saw the prisoner Daley standing at the window of the first-floor, with his coat off - the house is only one story high; O'Donnell was lying in bed, with all the clothes covered over him; the watchman pulled the clothes down, but he could not make any sense of O'Donnell; he pretended to be very much intoxicated - I said, directly I saw him, that he was the man; they took Daley to the watch-house, and me also; they went back, and brought O'Donnell -Cooper, the officer, the watchman, the patrol, and Finch, were there; they all went into the house; Wood was also in the house. I believe, when Daley and I came down stairs with the watchman, my things laid opposite the door - I saw my bonnet, handkerchief, shawl, and pattens there.
Cross-examained by MR. BARRY. Q. With whom do you live? A. With my brother and sister - they lodge there; they lived at first in Brunswick-street: I had moved with them from there to this house, a week before -I board and lodge with them: I am an unfortunate girl; I have lived at St. Luke's workhouse since this; the Magistrate thought proper to send me there - my brother and sister know where I am; I had left home about eight o' clock that evening, or between eight and nine.
Q. Were you asked at the office at what time you left home, and said, at twelve at night? A. No, I was never asked at what time I left home; there was a second hearing; I was not then asked at what time I left home -I do not recollect your asking me the question; twelve o' clock is generally the time I return - I had the shilling and sixpence in my handkerchief; I had not received that out of doors that night - I dined and had tea at home: I had been in company with a person before, on the other side of the water; I had only been in company with one; it was a young man; I cannot say what time I spent with him - I did not drink with him at all.
Q. You do not know how many hours you were in his company? A. I was not "hours" at all, nor yet an hour; I cannot say how long I was with him - O'Donnell was a perfect stranger to me; I never saw him before.
Q. Was not the man who spoke to you in Fleet-street, a person named Burke? A. No, O'Donnell is the man.
Q. When the patrol spoke to you, what was the reason you went away without waiting for your half-crown? A. The patrol said I had better go home, and I took his advice - I had not got the half-crown; I turned away, and the patrol saw me below the brewhouse; when O'Donnell followed me, I refused to return at first, and then went, as he said he would give me another shilling - I did not at first object to go for the half-crown; that I recollect - I will say nothing but the truth; I do not know that I objected at first to have half a crown; there is one gas-light in the court; I do not know whether there is more - I had never been there before; I spoke to no woman on the road; O'Donnell spoke to no woman but myself - O'Donnell put his hand on my shoulder, and tried to push me into a place; I was not pushed in, as I put my hand to the side of the door, and hindered them from getting me in - it is a public privy; I saw nobody about till I felt two men pulling me - I cannot swear to those men; Daley is as near the height of one of them as possible - he is a common-sized man.
Q. Are we to understand you were pushed by one, and pulled by two inside, and yet they could not get you in? A. I am not certain the two were inside: they did not get me in, for O'Donnell knocked me down in the court - I called out, when I found there was more than one - that was before a hand was put before my mouth, and O'Donnell after that cried out, "Stop the b-y b-cb's mouth.
Q. Did you not understand that was to stop your calling? A. I thought it might be so, till he put his hand over my mouth and nose; I heard nothing about a lark - no liberties had been taken with my person that night.
Q. Did you tell the Magistrate the persons you could not identify were pulling your legs and shoulders while you were on the ground? A. Yes, I did; the watchman and others searched for my things before I went into the house, and when we came out, they were found on the ground; the patten laid at a little distance, but the other things were thrown in a heap together, not tied up.
Q. How many people had assembled at that time? A. I did not see any; I did not notice a woman looking out of her window; I think there are houses only on one side of the court; when I went into the house, I said I thought Daley looked like one of the men; I did not say Sheridan, the landlord, was one - I spoke to nobody but O'Dounell; I said Daley looked like one, but I could not swear to him; when O'Donnell came into the watch-house I said I knew his voice, and he was the man; I did not say I knew Daley's voice; I did not speak to one Kenney.
JAMES McDONALD. I am a patrol. On the 30th of March, at a quarter before two o'clock in the morning, I was coming down Playhouse-yard: I heard some foot-steps at the end of Lion and Lamb-court, which is in St. Luke's,and in Middlesex; I called the wathchman to bring his light - he came with his light, and three or four men and one woman were in the entry; I asked the woman what she was doing there, she said she was brought there by one of the men; I told her she had better go home, and she walked away; I immediately asked the men if they lived there - they said they did live in that court; I told them it was time they should be at home and when I came down to the end of the yard, the girl was standing by a post, and asked me to direct her to Blackfriars-road; I knew three of the men - that was Daley, O'Donnell, and Burke; I knew them by seeing them come out of their place to go to work of a morning; I went a little way down the lane, with the prosecutrix, pointed to the bottom of the lane, and directed her home; I crossed over to the other side of the way, and while standing there I saw O'Donnell come out of Playhouse-yard, and go in the same direction as the girl had gone, and in two or three minutes I saw her and him returning up the lane again - walking arm-in arm; they came up to Playhouse-yard, and turned the corner again; and after turning the corner, there were three or four of the same party standing talking to the watchman on the opposite side of the way; and in the course of half a minute, they left him and proceeded up the court after the man and woman; I immediately proceeded over to another watchman, who was coming down the lane; I and two watchman went from the other side of the way to the corner of the yard, and heard a person screaming; we ran up Lion and Lamb-court, and saw the prosecutrix rising from the ground, apparently very much dejected, her hair very much disordered, and all over mud and wet, she told me something, and made her complaint to me; we went to search if we could find any of the property she had lost but found none - she pointed to a house, we went in, and while looking below (there was a man and his wife in bed) we heard a window pulled up, and footsteps up stairs; we immediately went up stairs and met Daley coming from towards the window - the prosecutrix then came up stairs and said that was one of them, that was Daley, and on looking round there were some more men asleep in a bed; she did not point out any of them, but on the third bed, on coming towards the door, we saw a heap on the bed, and on pulling the coverlid down we found O'Donnell underneath, with his clothes on; we tried to awake him, to get sense into him, but he appeared quite speechless, we could not get a word from him - the man and woman who were below came up stairs, and said he had not been out of bed since nine o'clock that morning; on taking hold of his hand to look at it, the sleeve of his jacket was all over wet mud - we took Daley into custody, and brought him out at the door, and on coming out of the door of the house the watchman pointed to the things that laid in the court; (there were three beds in the room, the prosecutrix did not recognise any body on the second bed); the watchman picked the things up and took them to the watch-house with Daley, and as we turned to come on I saw a handkerchief lying down, which I picked up, and told the girl to tie it round her neck, which was quite bare; and when we came just to the bottom of the court I met Cooper, the inspector; I delivered the girl to him, and remained close in sight of the premises till he returned again with the watchman - they went into the house and brought O'Donnell down, and took him to the watch-house - I remained behind; I know nothing more.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell the prosecutrix she had better go home? A. I did; she stood with O'Donnell close at the corner; the others were at a distance from them, at the turn of the court - she said one of the men had brought her there.
Q. Then of course she gave you to understand there were other men there? A. No.
COURT. Q. What were her words? A She said she had been brought there by one of the men: those were her words; that was not the first time, but when we went to her assistance - when I first went to her she said nothing about any men.
MR. BARRY. Q. She pointed out O'Donnell in bed? A. We turned the clothes down, and she said she could swear that was one of the men; we could not make him understand, speak, or move - she said Daley was one of the men, that was before we saw O'Donnell - the man and woman who came up were both undressed; there were seven inmates altogether - I do not know their names; I knew Sheridan by sight, as I had his brother in custody a little while ago; I did not know him by name till then - I have ascertained that he keeps the house; I have been a patrol these five years - I believe there are twelve houses in the court, several of them had their windows up and people looking out; I saw none of the inhabitants of the court down below except the people of that house; a window next door but one to the house was open, and a person looking out at No. 10 - the next house to that is empty; there is a small gutter in the court - I found the handkerchief close to the end of the gutter, it was a pocket handkerchief - I found one patten lying by the side of it.
COURT. Q. She pointed out Daley as one of the men? A. Yes; he was coming from the window, with his coat off - he was the first we saw on going into the room.
ROBERT WOODS. I am a watchman. On the morning of the 30th of March, about a quarter before two o'clock, the patrol called me for a light - I was opposite Playhouse-yard; I went up to Lion and Lamb-court with him, and saw O'Donnell, Daley and Burke, all three standing there together - I knew Burke before, but not the other two; I am certain of the prisoners - the patrol told the prosecutrix to go home; she went away -O'Donnell went after her in a few minutes, and I saw him come back with her; Burke came down Playhouse-yard over to me, and spoke to me - then Daley and two or three more, who were standing there, went up Playhouse-yard; I had spoken to them before that - I heard a scream of Watch! called out; I went up Lion and Lamb-court, where the scream came from, and the prosecutrix was just getting up; she pointed to No. 8 - I went in there with the patrol and another watchman; I saw Daley, who had just got his coat off, going to bed - several more were in bed there; I saw O'Donnell in bed with the clothes covered all over him - Coombs said Daley was one of them; we took him to the watch-house with her; as we went along Daley said it was not him, and said, "Here is a shilling for you to drink, it was not me, and I nudged you when you were there; it was O'Donnell;" O'Donnell was in bed covered with the clothes, and appeared to be very much in liquor; Coombs said at the watch-house that she could swear to him by his voice - she had pointed him out when he was in bed; when we came down, Finch picked up a shawl, bonnet, pair of pattens, and a handkerchief, and gave it to me to take to the watch-house - they were all rolled up together.
Cross-examined. Q. All rolled up together? A. There was a shawl, patten, and bonnet rolled up together when it was given to me; I did not see them picked up- I do not think Brown the watch-house keeper is here; he was examined at Worship-street, and sworn.
Q. Where were you standing at the commencement of this? A. Opposite Playhouse-yard; I had been walking about two or three minutes - I did not see the prosecutrix come up to the court the first time; I might be in another part of the lane - she did say she had been brought there by one of the men.
COURT. Q. When did she say so? A. I forget now; it was when I was up the court - I do not know whether it was after she was told to go home; I went up the court when I heard the screams.
WILLIAM FINCH. I am a watchman. I went to Lion and Lamb-court on Monday morning, the 30th of March, about ten minutes before two o'clock; the patrol came tome - I went into No. 8, and saw Daley standing by the window with his coat off; the girl gave charge of him, and I took him to the watch-house - I pulled the bedclothes down, and saw O'Donnell there dressed; I tried to rouse him - he pretended to be so intoxicated, no sense could be beat into him; the girl saw me pull the clothes down; I felt wet mud on his nose - the girl charged Daley first; I came back after taking him, and took O'Donnell to the watch-house; the girl pointed him out in the bed, but he seemed so drunk we could do nothing with him till after I took the other to the watch-house; I picked up the shawl in the court, and gave it to the patrol; I saw the bonnet lying in the gutter, but nothing else; I did not see the handkerchief - they were right opposite the door of No. 8.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is the gutter from the house? A. A very little distance - the court is very narrow; I found the shawl very near the gutter, the bonnet lying in the gutter, and a patten was found in the gutter, but I did not see it - I went to the watch-house; Coombs said nothing in my presence of knowing any body's voice.
WILLIAM EDWARD COOPER. I am an inspector of the watch. On the morning of the 30th of March, about two o'clock, I was in Playhouse-yard, and met Daley in the custody of Finch and Woods: I took the girl to the watch-house - I afterwards went to No. 8, Lion and Lamb-court, and saw O'Donnell in bed, with all his clothes on except his flannel jacket, and covered over with the bed clothes; I shook him, and told him it was of no use to play tricks, and if he did not come out of bed I would pull him out; he got out of bed, and put his flannel jacket on - he then put one of his boots on up stairs, and the other down; I took him to the watch-house - the minute he entered the watch-house he began to speak; the prosecutrix said,"That is one of them, I can swear to him by his voice" - she had not seen his face then; one of the arms of his jacket was wet, and the other was wet round the cuff; the prosecutrix's things were delivered to me at the watch-house- the bonnet and shawl were delivered to me by Brown, the watch-house keeper; the handkerchief was given to me by the prosecutrix - she had it round her neck when I took her to the watch-house; Brown is not here; I received them in the watch-house - the patrol gave me one patten in Playhouse-yard, and Brown the other; I have had them ever since - I found no money in the handkerchief; there was no knot in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there the slightest appearance of there having been a knot in it? A. It was as she gave it to me in the morning when she was going away to the office, just before eleven o'clock; Brown was not bound over to come here.
MR. BARRY to JAMES McDONALD. Q. Why did you not keep the handkerchief? A. Her neck was bare, and she had nothing on her head; nothing had been said to me about money being in it then; when I picked it up and gave it to her she did not ask me where the 1s. 6d. was -I picked that and a patten up close to the water-run; the things were nearly close together, but not quite.
COURT. Q. Did you see the other things lying about? A. They were all picked up before I got to them; I saw the shawl lying down, but, it being dark, I saw nothing else.
MARY CANNON. I live at No. 20, Playhouse-yard, which is five houses from Lion and Lamb-court - I know No. 8 there. On the morning of the 30th of March, after two o'clock, I saw the prosecutrix going into No. 8 with the watchman, and as soon as she entered the lower room I saw the one pair of stairs window open, and saw a bundle chucked out of the window, but what was in it I cannot tell - Daley chucked it out; I knew him before - I have often seen him in the court, and at the door; there was a candle on the window-ledge, so that I could see him; I am certain it was him - he had no coat on; it was a small sized bundle; I cannot say how large, because I only saw it by the light of the candle - I could not see where it fell, because there is a privy between my window and the house, so that I could not see the bottom of the court - I had to look sideways; my house is on the opposite side, four or five yards from No. 8; No. 7 is directly opposite to me - only one side of the court is called Playhouse-yard, and the other Lion and Lamb-court; I have not to turn a corner to go to the house.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you first give an account of this? A. On Tuesday I went to Cooper's house; I did not call out that a bundle was thrown out, because I did not know the girl had been robbed - I heard a scream; there was a stir in the court - I am a shoe-binder, and single; I have lived there nearly four years - I was in bed, but heard screams, and got up; I am an English woman - a great many Irish live in the court; I saw the prosecutrix in the watehman's hands, and could have called over to them if I had liked; they were not above five yards from me.
JURY. Q. When you saw the bundle thrown out, was the patrol outside or inside? A. Inside; I remained at the window till he and the prosecutrix came out - I spoke of this on Monday; but did not tell Cooper till Tuesday.
WILLIAM FINCH. I picked up a shawl, and gave it to McDonald, I think or Woods.
JAMES McDONALD. Finch did not give me the shawl; it was never in my possession.
ROBERT WOODS. The shawl, bonnet, and one patten were given to me, but I do not know who by, I am sure, in the bustle.
ELIZABETH COOMBS. This is my handkerchief; I know it by the colour - I have had it a good bit: I believe my sister hemmed it; when it was given back to me my money was not in it; I never thought about the money then - it had been tied up in one single knot; it could not have fallen out unless it was untied - the bonnet is mine; I trimmed and lined it myself; the shawl I know by a piece out of the corner of the border - I know the pattens; I had had them about a week or a fortnight: all the things are mine, and what I had on when I went into the court.
O'DONNELL'S Defence. I had nothing to do with her; I never raised my hand to her, nor hurt her; I was not near Blackfriars-bridge that night; I never left Golden-lane - I never went further than the lane: I was never in Fleet-street. The watchman says he took me out of bed in my clothes; I had my boots, jacket, and waistcoat off; I had nothing to do with the woman.
DALEY'S Defence. I know nothing about it, except as I came into the court I saw the watchman and this woman - I heard her call the watchman to show her a light; I saw her and Burke at the end of the court - the watchmanshowed her a light; she said she had got a farthing instead of a shilling from Burke - the watchman asked if we lived in the court; we said we did: he told us to go in and go to bed - I stood talking to the watchman, and know nothing more.
PATRICK KENNEY. I am a labouring man. On the 30th of March I lodged at No. 8, Lion and Lamb-court. About two o'clock in the morning the prosecutrix came into the house, and said I was the person who knocked her about outside; I had been in bed since between nine and ten o'clock, and did not get up till this affair; I am married, and my wife is in Ireland - she said, "That is the man."
COURT. Q. Was she pointing to you or to any body else? A. She did, and the watchman said, "He is not Burke;" I do not know the watchman; (looking at the witnesses) I cannot swear to any of them - I cannot say whether they were there that night; I think three watchmen came in with her - I have known O'Donnell two years and Daley half a year; they are labourers, and bore good characters.
ELIZABETH COOMBS. I do not recollect seeing this man; I swore to nobody but O'Donnell.
JAMES McDONALD. I went into the room with Coombs, and saw Kenney; Coombs did not speak of him at all; he was in his shirt - she said nothing to him.
WILLIAM FINCH. I went with Coombs; Kenney jumped out of bed in his shirt when we went into the room; Coombs made no charge against him in my hearing.
ROBERT WOODS. I do not recollect seeing Kenney there.
PATRICK SHERIDAN. I live at this house, and am the landlord. I was in bed with my wife and asleep; I was disturbed about half-past two o'clock, and the very moment Coombs came in she said, "This is one of the men - I will take my oath this is one of them:" I had been in bed since nine o'clock.
COURT. Q. Did she address herself to you, that you were one? A. Yes; the moment the watchman turned his lantern over me, she said, "That is one of them;" there was no light in my house before the watchman came.
JAMES McDONALD. Sheridan and his wife were in bed- Coombs did not charge him as being the man, or one of them; she did not speak to him down stairs, nor of him - the watchmen were looking about the house, but these people were in bed.
WILLIAM FINCH. Coombs did not charge Sheridan as being one of the men in my presence; I saw him in bed - two of us were up stairs, and one down.
ROBERT WOODS. I saw Sheridan that night: Coombs said nothing about him in my presence - the watchman were looking about in different places, some in one place, and some in another; Sheridan slept in the parlour, and Kenney up stairs.
PATRICK SHERIDAN. All the watchmen had lights. I have known the prisoners four years; they are honest hardworking men.
JAMES GHRIMES. I am a labourer, and lodge in this house. I was asleep up stairs on this night; the prosecutrix said nothing to me, but she gave charge of Sheridan and kenney, who slept in my bed - I have known the prisoners three years; they are honest men.
ELIZABETH COOMBS. I never got my 1s. 6d.; it could not have fallen out of the handkerchief it was tied in.
Six witnesses deposed of both the prisoners' good characters.
O'DONNELL - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 23.
DALEY - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
[trialAccount t18290409-287]
Before Mr. Justice Guzelee.
965. WILLIAM BAKER was indicted for that he, on the 21st of February, at St Andrew, Holborn, feloniously did falsely make, forge, and counterfeit a certain order for payment of money, which is as follows:
St. Ann's Union.
MR. THOMAS, - Sir, You will please to pay the bearer, for Richard Power, three pounds, for three weeks due to him, a country member, and you will much oblige, yours, &c.
2, Brown's-building, Stanhope-street, J. BESWICK, Mr. Thomas, Gray's Inn-lane. Feb. 21, 1829. with intention to defraud John Thomas, the said John Thomas, on whom the said false, forged, and counterfeited order for payment of money was made, then and there, being entrusted with, and having in his hands and possession, as the landlord of a certain public-house known by the sign of the Crown and Barley Mow. in Gray's Inn-lane, a certain large sum of money, to wit, 30l., belonging to William Ireland and others, members of a friendly society, called the St. Ann's Union, meeting at the said public-house, for the purpose of enabling the said John Thomas to pay, and that he might pay all orders for payment of money made by John James Beswick, the Secretary of the said Society, upon the said John Thomas, for claims made, and monies due to any of the members of the said society, according to the rules and regulations thereof; against the Statute, &c.
2d COUNT, that he, on the same day, at the same parish, feloniously did utter and publish as true, a certain false, forged, and counterfeited order for payment of money, well knowing the same to be false, forged, and counterfeited, which is as follows - (setting it out as before.) with intent to defraud the said John Thomas; against the Statute.
3d COUNT like the second, only stating the intent to be to defraud John James Beswick.
4th COUNT the same, only stating the intent to be to defraud William Ireland and others.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
JOHN THOMAS. I am landlord of the Crown and Barley Mow, Gray's Inn-lane. The St. Ann's Union Benefit Society is held at my house, Mr. Beswick is the secretary; he leaves money in my hand to pay any orders he may send for money; on Saturday, the 21st of February, I remember seeing the prisoner in front of my bar in the evening, between six and seven o'clock (my wife, who has seen him before, is not here) - he produced a cheque, and said he had brought that from Mr. Beswick for 3l. for a country member who had broken his arm - I looked at the cheque, and saw that it was not Beswick's hand writing; I told him it was not Mr. Beswick's hand-writing - he said he knew that, Mr. Beswick was out of town, at a funeral, being an undertaker, and that Mrs. Beswick directed her son to write it; I accordingly gave him the 3l., and put the cheque on the file of the society.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner himself produce that cheque to you or to your wife? A. He produced it to me;my wife called me down stairs to him - my wife delivered the cheque into my hands in his presence; when I came down. I saw the prisoner standing in front of the bar; my wife said, "This gentleman has brought a cheque from Mr. Beswick for 3l.;" he stood in front of the bar, and saw her give me the cheque - I looked at it, and told him it was not Mr. Beswick's hand-writing, and gave it back into his hand again: he told me he knew it was not Mr. Beswick who wrote it, that Mr. Beswick was out of town, and Mrs. Beswick directed her son to write it; I did not ask who he was.
Prisoner. That is not the person who gave me the money, it was his wife - he never gave the cheque into my hand. Witness. I gave it to him myself.
COURT. Q. You put the cheque on the file of the society, where was that kept? A. It hung up in the bar; the club meet on the 3d and 4th Monday in every month.
JOHN JAMES BESWICK. I am secretary to the St. Ann's Union Benefit Society, that is the title. I have a cheque which I got from Mr. Thomas - I have looked at the name"J Beswick," on it; it is not my hand-writing - there is no member named Richard Power, nor ever was; on the 21st of February I lived at No. 2, Brown's-buildings, Stanhope-street, Clare market - this "J. Beswick" is not my hand-writing - I know nothing whatever of the origin of it; I never authorised any body to sign it in my name or to issue it; I have a son named James and another named John, but he was not at home, nor was he on the 21st of February; he is in service in London - this " J. Beswick" is not the hand-writing of any of my family.
JAMES BESWICK. I am the son of John James Beswick, and was fourteen years old last January. I never wrote this order, and know nothing about it; it is not my writing.
WILLIAM IRELAND. I am a member of the St. Ann's Union, and was so on the 21st of February - there are other members besides me; I subscribe to the funds - if I am taken ill, or disabled, I receive support from the funds of the Society.
GEORGE SALTER. (Looking at the order.) I know the prisoner; I am a carpenter, and live at No. 6, Osborne-street, St. James' - I have seen the prisoner write a great many letters; I believe this order to be his hand-writing, but cannot swear that it is.
COURT. Q. How long have you known him? A. Nineteen years - he has been in my employ; he kept my books for the last twelve months - I had frequent occasion to see him write; I suppose the whole of the order to be his hand-writing - it is a good deal like his writing - I have trusted the prisoner to receive and pay money, 40l. or 50l. at a time; he always did it faithfully - he behaved honestly; he had been very much reduced till he came to my house - I used to employ him as well as I could.
JOHN THOMAS re-examined. This is the order I received from the prisoner, and put on the file of the Society, on the evening of Saturday, the 21st of February; I gave it to Beswick on the Monday following, in the evening - it had been on the file till then; nobody but my own family had access to the bar - I know it to be the same paper, because I put my hand-writing on it.
THOMAS GOOK. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner on the 4th of March, in Osborne-street, St. James', at Mr. Salter's house; I searched him after I got to the office, and found this paper, among a number of others; this induced me to go to Mr. Beswick's - I told him, both before I found the paper, and after, that he was charged with forging certain orders; he wished me to allow him to put that paper into the fire; I told him he had had plenty of time to destroy any thing he chose before I took him - I then placed him in the back office, among a number of other prisoners: I observed him watching me very closely - I had occasion to go into the Magistrates' room, and after returning from the Magistrates' room, I found he was gone from where I had placed him: the place he had been in was not locked; I immediately ran out of the office door, and observed him turning the corner into Blenheim-street, about fifty yards from the office - I followed, and overtook him; I have known him twenty years - he bore an excellent character.
The order was here read, as set out in the indictment; the paper found on the prisoner was verbatim the same, except not being headed "St. Ann's Society."
Prisoner's Defence. I am unprepared with Counsel, having had so little time; I never knew a word of this charge till I had a note from the solicitor on Saturday - the keeper of Clerkenwell prison did not know I was to be tried here; I understood it was for a fraud.
MR. PHILLIPS. The landlady is here, if the prisoner wishes to examine her.
Prisoner. I have nothing to ask her.
The Court inquired if the prosecutors were prepared to produce any rules of the Society, authorising the secretary to draw upon Mr. Thomas. - Mr. Phillips replied in the negative.
GUILTY (of uttering). Aged 56.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury, on account of his former good character, and his reduced situation.[This case stands reserved for the consideration of the Twelve Judges.]
There was another indictment against the prisoner for a similar offence, committed on the 11th of February, upon which no evidence being offered, he was ACQUITTED.
[trialAccount t18290409-288]
966. STANLEY SHARP was indicted for feloniously forging an acceptance to a certain bill of exchange, which is as follows: 18th September, 1828.
Two months after date pay to me, or my order, 7l. Accepted, John Pettit. WILLIAM PETTIT.
Mr. John Pettit, 17, Seymour-street, Bryanstone-square. with intent to defraud John Pettit; against the Statute.
SECOND COUNT, for uttering the same with a like intent.
EDWARD SAYER PETTIT. I am a draper, and live at Greenwich. I have a brother John, and one named William, who was unfortunate in business, and was in prison; he lived at Greenwich also - I know John Pettit, who lived in Seymour-place, Bryanstone-square; I produce a forged bill for 7l. On the 18th of September I saw my brother william draw a bill of the same date and amount exactly - the one I produce is the prisoner's hand-writing; the whole of it: he did live at Sheerness, but on the 18th of September he was a prisoner in the King's Bench, and was so on the 21st of November, when this bill became due - shortly before that I received 7l. from my brother William, which I gave to my mother; to pay the original bill with, that had been paid to the prisoner, but I am certain neither the signature, acceptance, nor the indorsements, William Pettit and Edward Sayer Pettit, are my brother's hand-writing - he had dealings with the prisoner, who was an attorney; the bill is receipted in the prisoner's writing.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Here is a receipt to the bill in the prisoner's writing? A. Yes, and the letters C.B.P.
Q. That means Copy of the Bill of Pettit's - why not tell us before, that those letters were there? A. I was asked about the indorsements; there is no stamp to it - my father did accept a bill, of which this is a verbatim copy, but without a stamp - the original has never been presented for payment, but we have bad a letter about it; this is the original bill (looking at one produced by the prisoner); my mother is nearly blind, and did not know but she was paying the original bill - I have a letter stating that the bill was not paid, and we must look to it; we received that the next evening.
Q. Well, it was not paid in full? A. All but 3s.; the original bill was given to the prisoner.
Q. Have there not been continual quarrels between your family and the prisoner, about law bills? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Was the letter written before the bill became due? A. After; the post-mark is the 22d of November, but the letter is not dated - one came the same morning from the holder of the bill; I and my brother William paid the original bill to the prisoner - he would not take it without my indorsement; there is a receipt for 7l. on the copy.
ANN PETTIT. I am the wife of John Pettit. I received 7l. from my son about the 17th or 18th of November - we at that time lived in Seymour-place, Bryanstone-square: the money was given to me to pay a bill; on the 21st of November the prisoner came to Seymour-place - I had not enough by 3s., and I paid him 6l. 17s., telling him he should have the remainder in the evening; he was in the Bench that day, but he came to me between eleven and twelve o'clock in the morning - he said he had brought the bill, and presented it to me; he said he came for payment of the bill, and next day I received this letter (looking at it) from him - I know it to be his hand-writing; he only said he had brought the bill for payment, and gave me this paper - I did not know but it was the original bill; nobody else was at home - my husband generally goes out for a walk between nine and ten o'clock, and returns about one; I saw the prisoner write this receipt on the back - he then went away; he had called on the 19th for it, but I told him I should not pay it till the three days grace were up - he had a paper in his hand then; what it was I do not know - if he had said the paper was only a copy I should not have paid him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he never sue your family for a bill of costs? A. No, not a just bill; he sued two of my sons, but not these sons; I did not look at the bill, for knowing him to be the holder, I thought it was all right.
COURT. Q. Did you look at it when he brought it? A. No, he laid it on the table; I doubled it up, and put it away; but next morning, I found it was not the right bill - we have had a letter from one Rogers, demanding payment of the original bill; the prisoner came twice before, I paid it; he never sent for the 3s.; we sent to the King's Bench to get the bill, but he would not give it up.
WILLIAM JOHN REYNOLDS. I am an agent. I went to Greenwich to settle an account with William Pettit, a few days after this was paid; Pettit gave me two letters, one signed Rogers, and the other Sharp; I went to his mother, and she gave me the copy of the bill - I found the prisoner in the strong-room of the King's Bench; I shewed him this piece of paper, and asked if he had not received 7l.; he said No, not 7l., but that the paper was his handwriting; I said it was not William Pettit's wish to do any thing against him, provided he gave up the original bill-he said,"I cannot, for I have paid it away to a Mr. Rogers;" at that time Rogers was coming up stairs, and said, "Who makes use of my name;" Sharp said, "This person has come about that bill of Pettit's;" Rogers said, "I have given value for it, and if not paid to-day, I will sue all the parties;" I went to Sharp a day or two after, and said he had better give up the bill, and Pettit would drop all proceedings; he said he could not; and said, "if you look on the back of the bill. you will see C.P.B., which means Copy of Pettit's Bill," but it is C.B.P.; Edward Pettit and I went to him again a week or ten days after; he said,"I have come up with instructions from my brother to get a warrant, but if you will give up the bill, nothing shall be done;" he said he could not do it, and a warrant was lodged for a fraud.
Cross-examined. Q. He could have avoided all proceedings by handing you back the original bill? A. Yes, I told him so; Pettit and him have not been at law - he was employed by a married sister of Pettit's - I live in Drummond-street, Euston-square; sometime after the warrant was lodged, he shewed me the original bill; he said he had got it back, and would send a person to meet Pettit about it, which he did.
JOHN PETTIT. I lived in Seymour-place. The signature to this bill is not my writing, nor is the name William Pettit my son's writing; the body of the bill is not written by either of us.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prisoner ever take proceedings against your and your sons George, William, and Edward? A. No: Mr. Barnard is my son-in-law; he might take proceedings against two of his sons, but not against three, nor against me.
The prisoner, in his defence, stated that he had been engaged in law proceedings for William Pettit, who had compounded with his creditors, and paid him 7l. in discharge of his claim, and he was in the habit of copying all his bills; on the day in question he called for payment, and as Mrs. Pettit had not the whole amount, he left her the copy, stating that he would give up the original when the balance was paid; that the letter produced was not dated, and might have been written before it was due, and that he had repeatedly offered to give the bill up on receiving the balance.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-289]
First Middlesex Jury. - Before Mr. Recorder.
967. JOHN HARRIS was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Towers, on the 14th of November, at Christchurch, and stealing therein 36 yards of trimming, value 7s.; 20lbs. of cotton, value 3l.; 10lbs. of worsted, value 30s.; 32 pieces of ribbon, containing in length 36 yards each, value 15s., the goods of the said John Towers.
JOHN TOWERS. I live at No. 46, Phoenix-street, in theparish of Christchurch, Spitalfields, and am a silk-manufacturer - I rent the house, and know the prisoner; he was never in my employ. On the 14th of February, about six o'clock in the evening; I went out to Wood-street, on business - I left my housekeeper, Maria Pritchard, in the house; the house door is kept shut at that time of day - I returned at half-past seven o'clock, and found things the same as I had left them, but I did not examine the warehouse at that time, and am not aware of any thing being gone, as the doors were closed as I had left them; the warehouse is part of my dwelling-house: after my return, I was sitting by my fireside, in the room over the warehouse, and thought I heard something like a bolt snap; I sent my son down with a light, to see what it was, as I was very tired - I trusted to what he said: I went down to the warehouse about half-past eight o'clock, and missed thirty-six yards of trimming, value 7s. 6d., and 20 lbs. of cotton, worth 3l., 10lbs. of worsted, worth 30s., and thirty-three pieces of ribbon, of thirty-six yards each, worth 15l.; I am quite certain all these things were safe when I was last in the warehouse - I saw twenty-four yards of one of the pieces of ribbon about a month afterwards, at a public-house in Wheeler-street; I was quite certain it was my goods - the prisoner was not then in custody: I found my back door had been forced open: it had been locked - I observed some grease just where the bolt went, and the mark of some instrument having forced it - the bolt of the lock was forced from the socket; I had left the warehouse at six o'clock, and gave my boy the key to lock the door; I locked the front door, but not the back.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. When had you seen the property safe? A. At the time I went out; I am sure the drawers were secure, and the goods in them; I had seen them in the course of the day - no persons but customers have access to the warehouse; I am in a small way of business - I may have six customers in the course of the day: the things could not be taken without forcing the door; I have seen some cotton and silk the same colour as I lost, but I cannot swear to that.
JOHN TOWERS. JUN. I am fourteen years old next month. On this day my father went out about six o'clock, leaving nobody that was engaged in the business but myself; the back door of the warehouse was open when my father left - I shut it directly; he went and locked it - the back door opens into a yard - a person could easily get to the yard without going through the house; all was safe then - I locked the doors, gave the light to the servant, and went out directly; the street door was locked also - nobody could get in except by force; the servant could not open the street door, for I took the key; I returned with my father about half-past seven o'clock and went up stairs - I was playing with the baby, and my father thought he heard a noise; I went down with a light, and found the back door shut and locked the same as when I left it; I did not examine the warehouse - I went up stairs again; my father afterwards sent me down stairs to fill the tea-kettle - the kitchen is on the same floor as the warehouse - and as I went by I saw the back door about half way open; I called to my father, went into the warehouse directly, and perceived some drawers lying on the counters - they had been in their places before; all the things were gone out of them; they were the drawers from where the silk or bobbins were taken - my father missed the property; the ribbons were kept in the drawers on the left-hand side - those drawers were not taken out of their places; I had the key of the back door in my pocket - nobody could have got in without forcing it; I know the prisoner, he came to the warehouse that same evening, about two hours before I left the house, to pay me 5s. which he owed my father for cottons he had bought; he knew the house, and often came there - he used to come into the warehouse.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did he stay in the warehouse that night? A. Two or three minutes; he has staid as long as that before; I did not suspect any harm of him; I locked the front and back door - the servant could go down a flight of stairs and go out without coming to the warehouse; it is a large house - there are four rooms and a shop.
Q. A noise made in one part might easily be heard in the other? A. Yes; at night the servants were up stairs.
JOHN APPLEBY. I live at No. 9, Vine - court, Lamb-street, Spitalfields, which is about ten minutes walk from the prosecutor's. I knew the prisoner - I saw him about three weeks after the robbery; he asked me to dispose of two pieces of work for him - he is a weaver; the work was silk and cotton - I had them to sell for him - they are hatters bands; I have a brother in the business, and told him I must send them to him - he told me to do so; my brother would not have them as they were not perfect work; I went and tried to sell them - in two or three days I exchanged one of the pieces with Mr. Holmes, of Whitechapel; I was sent for, and went to the public-house Towers has mentioned, and there shewed him a piece of hatters band which Towers claimed; I had received it from the prisoner, and told him so.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner's business? A. Yes; he is a silk-weaver, and has manufactured his own work; I have known him manufacture hatters' hands - that is never called ribbon; Towers had heard I had the goods to sell - he did not suspect me.
JURY. Q. Were you in the habit of seeing the prisoner frequently before the robbery? A. I had not seen him for fifteen months before, and then he was in my employ for two or three years.
Q. Is there any difference between these goods and what he made? A. He could manufacture such goods; I cannot tell the difference between his manufacture and another.
ELIZABETH FOUNTAIN. I am twelve years old. (This witness appeared perfectly to understand the nature of an oath.) I live at No. 45, Phoenix-street, with my father and mother - I know the prisoner, but not before the robbery; I live next door to Mr. Towers - I am not sure he is the man I saw; Towers' house was broken open on the Saturday night, and on that night I saw this young man come from up the court; I am not sure he is the man - he came from up the court and went across the street (Towers' back door opens into that court) - it was a little after eight o'clock; he had a bundle under his arm; I saw a man with a long brown great coat come up the court about two or three minutes after - he came from the same court as the person whom I took to be the prisoner - it was the first one who had the bundle; I saw a man with a red coat and a shiny hat walking up and down by the prosecutor's street-door; that man went towards Brick-lane, the other two wentup Farthing-street - I did not follow either of them; I was cleaning the threshold of the door - I saw Towers and his son that night, and told them what I had seen; I told Towers how the man with the bundle was dressed - which was in a blue coat, but that I was not sure that I should know him again; I saw the prisoner at Worship-street, and told the gentlemen that I was not sure it was him - and that I did not think it was him.
JURY. Q. Is the court a thoroughfare? A. No; there are about eight houses - the doors of which open to the court.
DANIEL HICKS. I am shopman to Mr. Holmes, a hatter, of Aldgate. I bought some trimmings of Appleby; they were silk and cotton hat-bands - the officer received them from me.
THOMAS WATERS. I am a Police-officer of Worship-street. On the 17th of March I took the prisoner at his own house in Princes-court, Gibraltar-walk, Bethnal-green; I asked him if he had sold any ribbon or hat-hands to Appleby - he said he had; I asked him where he got it - he said he found it about a fortnight ago on Farthing-hill; I shewed him one of them; I told him I should take him on suspicion of the robbery - Towers was with me; he denied the charge - I went up stairs to the room which he said he lived in, and found a quantity of cotton and a small quantity of silk, some in skeins and some on bobbins; he said they were all his own, and he had bought them; I told him he had better produce the bill of parcel before the Magistrate; the prosecutor said he had lost things of the quality, but would not swear to them; he identified the pieces of hands before the Magistrate - I got one of them from Hicks, and the other from the prosecutor himself.
JOHN TOWERS. These hat-bands were manufactured by a person I employed; I know the make, because it is one colour on the edge, and another on the body of the work; it was returned to my hands by Appleby's brother a month before the robbery - it was damaged, which was the reason I had them laying by me; I am certain they were in my own possession before the house was broken open - the person who manufactured them is not here; I had seen it that day in a large drawer, with thirty-two pieces, which were taken at the same time (looking at the hat-band); the drawer they were in was about half way open, and the lock hanging on one screw; it was in its proper state when I left the house - the prisoner's father was taken into custody, and discharged; he had never worked for me - some things were found in a room in the father's house, but the prisoner said he rented it of his father.
MR. CHURCHILL. Q. How many locks of drawers had been forced open? A. Only one; it had been done without much noise, for they had laid mats on the floor, to prevent their being heard - a noise would not be heard on the top story; we had a suspicion of the very man who manufactured these, but did not have him taken up; he had an opportunity of knowing the premises - he did not work for me at the time, and did not come above once a week - it is two years since he worked for me; he then worked in my shop - what I speak to this by is the difference in colour; it was a mistake of mine in putting two colours in - I never saw such before, nor has any other man I should think; I heard the noise about eight o'clock- I only heard one noise.
JURY. Q. Had you sold them to Appleby's brother? A. He ordered it of me, and returned it on my bands on account of the badness of colour; and after the robbery they were taken to his brother for sale; he knew it, and gave me information.
JOHN APPLEBY re-examined. Q. Is that the band you received from the prisoner, and offered to your brother, which he rejected? A. This is one of them - I did not take them to my brother, but sent them to him; he did not choose to have them; I could tell they were not well manufactured the instant I saw them; I shewed them to several hatters, who rejected them, as being badly made.
Cross-examined. Q. Is that the first badly manufactured article you ever saw? A. No, nor the last.
JURY. Q. Did you ever see any with the same defect in it? A. Never exactly, but very similar; it would be foolish to manufacture as this is - the body of the work is good, but the edges are another colour, thick, and clumsy; it is possible to make the same mistake.
JOHN TOWERS. The value of the property taken is 20l. and upwards; the articles mentioned are worth about 20l.
Prisoner. I leave my Defence to my Counsel.
JAMES ABERY. I remember the 14th of February. I saw the prisoner at half-past three o'clock that afternoon, and until eight in the evening, at the Half Moon public-house, in Bacon-street, Bethnal-green, which is no great way from Phoenix-street; I can walk it in five minutes - nothing particular occurred to make me remember the date; I left the place at eight o'clock, leaving him behind me there.
COURT. Q. What day was it? A. It was on a Saturday - I do not wear a watch; Spitalfield's church bell rings at eight o'clock, and it rang as I was going home; I left as it was ringing; it always strikes out at eight - I left while it was sounding.
ROBERT LOBB. I am a broad silk-weaver. I know the prisoner, and saw him on the 14th of February, about half-past six o'clock in the evening, at the Half Moon; I found him there when I went in - I staid there till ten- he went away about eight, while the bell was ringing.
JURY. Q. How long was it before you saw him again? A. The next Saturday - I have known him six years.
WILLIAM LOBB. I am brother to the last witness. I saw the prisoner at the Half Moon; I was with my brother - I went there about a quarter before seven, and remained there with the prisoner till a little after eight o'clock; he then went away - it might be five minutes past eight; the bell rings till a quarter past eight.
COURT. Q. Did you look at the public-house clock? A. Yes, I am quite sure of that; I generally look at the clock - the prisoner went away about five minutes, or it might be two or three minutes after eight; the Half Moon is about one hundred or one hundred and fifty yards from the prosecutor's house.
ROBERT BENNETT. I am a broad silk-weaver. I saw the prisoner at the Half Moon on the 14th of February; I went about half-past six o'clock, and he went away near upon eight.
COURT. Q. Do you mean a little short of eight? A. About that - I am certain be staid till eight.
ELIZABETH SMITH. My husband is a silk-manufacturer. I have known the prisoner nearly six years, and he bore a very good character; I saw him at my house on the 14th of February; I live at No. 19, Fashion-street, Bethnal-green.
Q. At what time did you see him? A. My husband had just come home from work; it had just gone eight o'clock when he came and knocked at our door, and remained there with my piece, while I went to market, and stopped with me, when I came back, till twenty minutes past nine; I then went with him to his mother's, to lend her my cloak.
Q. At what time did you go out to market? A. Just as the bell had done ringing eight - he came in as I went out: my house is about two hundred yards from the Half Moon; the bell generally rings a quarter of an hour - I left him in the house, and found him there when I returned.
COURT. Q. On what day was this? A. Saturday, about a month before he was taken up; I was at Worship-street when he was examined - but was not called in; I recollect it was Saturday, the 14th, because my niece's birth-day was the next day - I do not recollect that Saturday by any particular name; he frequently visited us, being a neighbour's child - he came two or three times a week; I had nothing particular to induce me to recollect the day more than I have said.
Q. If he was in the habit of calling on other days, why do you recollect that day? A. Yes; but that night he did not wish me good night, as I had to go to his mother's to take her a cloak; I do not keep my niece's birth day - I did not invite any friends.
Q. What then makes you recollect it was the Saturday before her birth-day? A. I heard of this robbery being done on the following week - I did not hear he was suspected for a month after; I have no particular reason for recollecting his calling that night - he called on other Saturdays; his mother came that day to ask me to lend her a cloak, to go to their uncle's next morning - that was about six o'clock in the evening - she had never borrowed it before.
Q. What enables you to say it was that particular Saturday? A. I have no particular reason; she returned it on Monday the 16th; I do not generally know the date of the month - I did not invite the prisoner or his mother to come on my niece's birth-day.
Q. Then is there any thing particular to lead you to remember the day, besides it being the day before your niece's birth-day? A. No - only she named to me that it was that day; the prisoner's mother told me it was Saturday the 14th; I have no reason for believing it was then, except from what she told me; the prisoner was taken up on a Tuesday, and after that his mother told me it was the day she borrowed the cloak.
Q. You would not have recollected it unless the mother told you? A. No - I should not.
HARRIET OSBORNE. Q. Do you remember last Valentine-day, as you are a young woman? A. Yes, it was the 14th of February; the prisoner came to our house that day as the bell was ringing eight o'clock, and staid till nine, or a few minutes after; he never went out during that time: my aunt came in from market, and then I went out.
COURT. Q. When was your birth-day? A. On Sunday, the 15th; the prisoner often visited me - he was paying his addresses to me; he generally came on Saturdays more than on other days - but not at my aunt's house.
Q. Where then did you meet him? A. Not particularly on Saturday - I was not always at my aunt's; I lived there - I do not recollect his visiting me any where else; he sometimes met me at the corner of the street - not always at my aunt's; I recollect this day, because it was Valentine's-day, and my birth-day was the 15th - he did not come the next Saturday, not that I recollect; he did not come every Saturday.
Q. Did you miss him on any Saturday? A. Yes, but not on any particular Saturday.
Q. What makes you remember this Saturday? A. Because next day a neighbour's daughter told me Towers had been robbed overnight - she lives next door to Towers: I did not tell any body the prisoner was with me that night, because nothing was said about him - I heard of his being taken up on the day he was taken, and said he was with me from a few minutes past eight o'clock till nine; I told a good many people so - I told my aunt; I did not see him on the Sunday, as I went on the Sunday morning with a neighbour's daughter to Woolwich; I did not come home till Tuesday night, and then heard he was taken - I went to Worship-street, and saw him getting into the van: I told my mother and a great many more that he was with me on the night of the robbery - I did not tell any body at the office so; I did not know any one - I went to Clerkenwell on the Friday, the day after he was examined; I knew when he was to be examined again, but did not go to the office - my aunt went, and I staid at home; I did not think it would be of any use for me to go - I stopped at home to do the cleaning; my aunt went - she was not called - she knew I was in his company from eight o'clock to nine.
Seven witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury on account of his good character.
