971. JANE CHAPPEL was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 pewter pot, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Mark Cullingford.
MARK CULLINGFORD. I am a publican, and live in York-street, Marylebone. The prisoner was brought to my house with this pot, which is mine; she was quite a stranger.
CHARLES SAGE. On Thursday night last, I was informed a woman had taken a pot from the door; I went out, and followed the prisoner up William-street - I took her with this put under her shawl.
Prisoner's Defence. I stopped to tie up my stocking, a man and a woman came by; the man put his face close to mine, and I took up the pot which stood there merely to pretend that I stooped for that - I had not gone many yards when this gentleman took me.
GUILTY. Aged 29.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18290409-293]
972. MARY CONNELLY was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 2 half-crowns, the monies of John Manuel.
JOHN MANUEL. I met the prisoner between nine and ten o'clock, on the 30th of March, in Sutton-street, Soho; I did not know her before; she said, "Well my dear," or something to that effect; we went into a passage together, but were not there more than half a minute; I then put my hand to my pocket, and my money was all correct - I had four half-crowns and 2s.; she said,"Let us go up stairs;" we went up, and she began to feel about my person, as we were on the first landing-place; we did not go into any room; I heard some money chink in her hand - I put my hand into my pocket, and missed two half-crowns; I accused her of the theft, and she dropped one halfpenny down the stairs, offered me another, and said there was my money; I said it was not, for I had lost two half-crowns; we came down stairs, and she called the watchman; I held her till the watchman came and searched her, but no half-crowns us were found; they had been in my right-hand waistcoat pocket, and could not have fallen out - I am quite sure she took them out; I had no halfpence about me; she said something about a shilling as we were going along; I was sober.
JOHN FRENCH. I am a watchman. I searched the prisoner as far as decency would allow; but found nothing on her - the prosecutor was quite sober, and stated the same as he has now.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going for some starch; he dragged me on the stairs; I said it was not the first time he had accused me wrongfully; he said he would transport me; I had a 1d. in my hand - he said on the stairs he would give me so and so: he had me here before for 4s. a year and a half ago, when I first came from Ireland; he was dragging me along, and the halfpence fell from me.
JOHN MANUEL. Upon my oath, I never saw the girl before; I never was before a Magistrate before in my life; there were a number of people round her.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18290409-294]
973. CHARLES DUNN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 ham, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Barford.
GEORGE ALLEN. I know Mr. Barford, a cheesemonger of East-street. About ten minutes before ten o'clock, on the 9th of April, I saw the prisoner and another come by- the other lad crossed, took this ham, and gave it to the prisoner, who put it under his coat, and walked off with it; a neighbour gave information to the prosecutor, who came out and pursued him; I saw the prisoner throw down the ham in Spring-street, and the watchman took him.
GEORGE HAWKER. I am a watchman. I took took the prisoner.
THOMAS BARFORD. This is my ham.
GUILTY. Aged 21.

974. JOHN HUNT was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 coat, value 5s. the goods of Richard Edwards.
RICHARD EDWARDS. I drive a cabriolet. I lost my coat on the 5th of April, out of my master's stable, in John-street, Tottenham-court-road; the door was shut but not locked - the gates were locked; I left it safe about half-past ten o'clock at night - I have seen the prisoner in the yard several times - he went out two or three times as odd man for my master; he knew the yard very well - there were five or six coats taken at the same time, and I believe ten whips: I found mine on Salter.
CHARLES SALTER. I am a hackney-coachman. I bought this coat of the prisoner last Monday week, in the evening - he wanted 5s., but agreed to let me have it for 3s. 6d.; it is old and torn.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I bought three coats and ten whips in the Old Bailey, on the Saturday night, of a man in a New market cut-off coat; I sold two coats to a Jew.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years.

975. JAMES KNOWLES was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of February, 5 sovereigns and 1 half sovereigns, the monies of William Povey.
WILLIAM POVEY, JUN. I drive the Stroundwater coach. Mr. Lawerence asked me to bring this money to Mr. Van, of Tooley-street: the prisoner had acted as porter to the coaches at the Bull inn, to which I came - I did not hear from Mr. Van, and on the Sunday morning I sent the prisoner to him to know if he would come; he returned and said, Mr. Van said if I would send the money over by him he would pay him - I gave him five sovereigns and one half-sovereigns, to take to Mr. Van; he did not return, and I wrote to Mr. Van to know if he had seen him.
Prisoner. Q. Have I not a great many times been intrusted with your money? A. Yes.
THOMAS VAN. The money never came to my hands; some of my family desired the prisoner to receive the money, but I was gone to church - I do not know whether the prisoner is the person who came; I do not know that any person authorised him to go for it on my account.
NOT GUILTY.

976. JOHN PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 3 pairs of pinching-irons, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Thomas Gregory.

[trialAccount t18290409-298]
977. MARY WELLS, TAMER SEETON, and ELIZABETH BERRY were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 gown, value 5s., the goods of John Russell Law.
CHARLES MILLS. I live with Mr. John Russell Law, a pawnbroker of Commercial-road. On the 8th of April Seeton and Wells came to our shop to pawn a dressing-case, which I would not take in - this gown was hanging up in the shop, but I did not observe them go near it; they came to the counter - the gown was near the counter; when they were gone I missed it; my suspicion fell on the two women - I went out, and found Berry about seven doors off with the gown under her shawl, at the corner of Berners-street; she had not been to the house - I said she must come with me to the shop; she said she was to have 6d. for carrying it, and pointed us to two women, but I did not observe them - I did not hear her say the word"Women;" I said before the Magistrate that she said two women had given her 6d. to carry it - she did say so; I brought her back to the shop; the officer took the other woman.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Berry mentioned that two women had given it her? A. I did not observe them - I did not see her point; she told me she pointed - there were two other persons in the shop, but they remained till I got back.
COURT. Q. Is it a long counter? A. Yes; it might have been taken by one without the other knowing it - I could not see who took it.
JAMES FOGG. I took Berry in charge with this gown; she said, "I did not steal it; Wells gave it to my sister Tamer, and she gave it me." I then went to Seeton's mother, and found Seeton there; I told her I wanted her and Mrs. Wells - she began to cry, and said "You have got Bet at the office for stealing a gown; but I am the thief, and she shall not suffer innocently - I took the gown from Laws, and they know it;" there was no promise or threat made to her - I had not been in the house a moment.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean to say she told you this story without your saying any thing to induce her? A. Yes; I knew her before, and she knew I was an officer - I believe she has three or four children.
JOHN ADAMS. I am an officer. We went, and took Wells after we had taken Seeton - Wells denied all knowledge of it; Seeton and Berry are sisters.(Property produced and sworn to.)
WELLS' Defence. I am innocent - I never saw the people till I went to the office.
SEETON's Defence. I am innocent.
JOHN MILLER. I am a waterman. I was standing at the corner of Berners-street talking to Berry, Seeton came up and said, "I have bought a gown Betsey, will you carry it for me under your shawl, and it will not get wet;" she took it, and they walked down the street.
Q. Do you live with either of them? A. I live close by- we have drank together; nothing was said about a sixpence - it rained very hard.
Q. She said she had bought the gown? A. Yes, for 4s.; they are sisters - I did not see Wells there; Berry desired me to come here - I was not before the Magistrate.
SEETON - GUILTY. Aged 26.
BERRY - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
WELLS - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-299]
978. ANN CORDWELL, THOMAS PURDY, and GEORGE HILL were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 3 sheets, value 11s; 2 petticoats, value 2s.; 6 handkerchiefs, value 2s.; and 1 shift, value 1s., the goods of the overseers of the poor, for the time being, of the parish of Fulham .
SECOND COUNT, stating them to be the property of Sarah Battershill.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
SARAH BATTERSHILL. I am matron of Fulham work-house . On the 16th of March the three prisoners were paupers there: I was absent for some time on parish business - I left the prisoners there, and when I returned they had absconded, about ten minutes, as I heard; I did not miss any property till the Tuesday night, when the officer took one of the prisoners, with a bundle; I knew the property - it had been in my care in the work-house.
ELIZABETH CHARLES. On Monday, the 16th of March, I saw Stokes, Cordwell, and Purdy, (but not Hill) with a bundle, which they all assisted to carry from the workhouse to the Rose and Crown public-house, at Putney; Stokes and Cordwell pawned some sheets at Mr. Steward's; we then went to the King's Arms; Stokes and I went to Mr. Butcher's, a pawnbroker's, at Wandsworth, and pawned a shirt for 2s., three flannel petticoats for 1s. 6d., a shift for 8s., and a handkerchief for 1s.; we then went back to the King's Arms public-house, and all four of us had breakfast; Cordwell and Purdy were there - they knew how we had disposed of the things, but Hill was not there at all; an officer came and took them into custody, and took Purdy with a bundle, but he did not take me; I went and gave information; Hill was in the house, but I do not know that he did any thing; I did not see these people steal the things.
JOSEPH ROSE. I am a constable. On the 17th of March I saw Purdy at Fulham, and took him, with a bundle -I asked how he came by it - he said I had better see; I shewed it to the matron.
ISAAC HAWKINS. I went to Mr. Steward's, and got two sheets belonging to the poor-house; Mrs. Battershill took me there; I then went to Mr. Butcher's, and got these other articles.
THOMAS ASHBY. I know the poor-house. On the 16th of March, about six o'clock in the evening, I saw Elizabeth Charles, Stokes, and Purdy, on the top of the premises - Hill was at the bottom, throwing up the bundles to Purdy; the matron was out of the way at the time; the prisoners left the house the same evening.(Property produced and sworn to.)
CORDWELL's Defence. We were washing on Monday afternoon; after tea, Stokes asked us to go away, and said she would get us work, and keep us till we got it; Charles then went into the yard, and got the linen - they were put in bundles, and at night we got over the wall: we went to Putney, and there they pawned two sheets; we then went to Wandsworth - we slept at the King's Arms; on the Monday morning they pawned one sheet at Mr. Butcher's, and two flannel petticoats, one shirt, and three hankerchiefs; Charles pawned the handkerchiefs in her own name, and Stokes the rest: we went to Chelsea next day, and then Stokes took two old sheets, and sold them, but I do not know to whom: on the Tuesday they ran away, and I never saw them till Thursday, when I saw Stokes, and asked what she had done with the duplicates; she he had said, burnt them; I was away a fortnight, and then I thought I had better give myself up, and suffer a little, and give up the company I was in.
PURDY's Defence. Stokes asked if I would run away? I said, I had such a bad jacket; she said, "I will buy you a jacket in Chelsea;" Stokes tied up the bundles; I saw them take three pieces of soap, which they sold in Jew's-row; they then went to the Rose and Crown public-house, and took the shirts - I was going away, but they would not let me.
HILL's Defence. I was down in the garden while the other prisoners were getting over; I asked where they were going? they did not answer; I never went away.
CORDWELL - GUILTY. Aged 30.
PURDY - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
HILL - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-300]
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
979. JOHN TAYLOR and BENJAMIN TAYLOR were indicted for breaking and entering a certain building, within the curtilage of the dwelling-house of Edward Chattaway, and occupied therewith, but not being part thereof, on the 11th of April, and stealing therein 5 live tame fowls, price 16s., his property.
MR. DOWLING conducted the prosecution.
EDWARD CHATTAWAY. I keep the workhouse at Edmonton . On the 10th of April I had some fowls in a hen-house at the corner of the workhouse yard; it is surrounded with a wall nine or ten feet high; on the 11th I missed five fowls, which I had seen the day before - I have not seen them again; the tiles of the hen-house were taken off: they had tried to get in by the door, and could not. I saw the prisoners apprehended; Benjamin Taylor had a plaid cap on.
Prisoner BENJAMIN TAYLOR. Q. Is there not more than one cap of a kind? A. Yes - I cannot say that I ever saw you with a cap on before; your brother wore one similar so it.
ELIZABETH HART. (This witness being deaf and dumb, gave her evidence through a person who could communicate with her.) I am a pauper in the workhouse at Edmonton; I know both the prisoners - they had been paupers there: on Saturday morning last I got out of bed and looked out of the window - it was a beautiful morning; I saw one of the prisoners on the wall, and the other on the ground, within the wall; there was another person with them, whom I did not know - one of the prisoners had a fowl, with its beak in his hand; the wings fluttered and he put it under his arm - I am quite sure the prisoners were two of the men: I rapped at the window, and described the prisoners to the master; Benjamin had a striped cap on his head.
Prisoner BENJAMIN TAYLOR. Q. Did you see me on the top of the wall? A. I saw him getting over the wall, I am quite certain - I did not see him take the tiles off.
JOHN CAMP. I am a constable. I took Benjamin Taylor, with this cap on his head; I have seen him wear it before.
JOHN TAYLOR 'S Defence. I was in bed and asleep at the time.
JAMES TAYLOR. I am the prisoners' father; they lodge at my house. On the night of Friday last I went home at half-past eight o'clock, and inquired if Benjamin had come in - my wife said he had: while I was eating my supper John came in, had his supper, and went to bed - when I got up the next morning, at six o'clock, they were both in bed: my house is a mile from the prosecutor's.
- TAYLOR. I am the prisoners' sister. My brother Benjamin was at home when my father came in; John then came in, and sat down - when they went to bed I locked the door, and took the key out; when I got up in the morning they were in bed - the key had laid on a chair in my bed room.
MR. DOWLING. Q. Were you ever in the House of Correction? A. Yes, once, for a few potatoes, and once for some gooseberries, which I had given to me.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-301]
980. JOHN GOODRICK and ROBERT GOODRICK were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Harry Phillips on the 3d of April, and stealing therein 12 drinking-glasses, value 18s.; 12 plates, value 12s.; 5 dishes, value 10s., and I looking-glass, value 10l., the goods of the said Harry Phillips .
MR. CHURCHILL conducted the prosecution.
MARY ANN BROWN. I am servant to Mr. Harry Phillips, and had the care of his house, which is Brandenburgh college, at Fulham, Middlesex. On the night of the 2d of April I locked up the house all safe - the next morning I went to open it, and found the door open and a chair against it; the shutters were put too, but the padlock which I had put on at night was broken off and gone; I looked in the kitchen, found the drawers all open, and the china and glass gone, which I had seen right the night before - I missed the chimney-glass from over the drawing-room fire-place, where I had laid the fire the day before; this iron bar laid across the fire-place, it appears to be the same the house was broken with.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was there any other servant in the house? A. No one slept in the house- I locked it outside, and went away; Mr. Phillips did not live in the house at all - it was left in my care: Mr. Phillips makes use of it at different times - he frequently drank tea there, and used those glasses when he was there.
JOSEPH PALMER. I know the two prisoners - they live at Hammersmith: I went to their house with a search warrant, on the 3rd of April, with a man of the name of Hallett and Mr. Phillips - I went up stairs, and was going into the room on the first floor, but was prevented by John Goodrick and his wife, who said there was a woman who had lain in but a few days - I said I must insist upon it; they begged me to wait a few minutes while they got the room fit for a man to go into - I placed a man at the door, and went up stairs; I found no property relating to this indictment; I came down again, and with very great reluctance they let me go into the room - in fact I was obliged to force my way in; I proceeded to search every part till I came to the bed: I lifted up one corner of the bed, and saw the mattress - I then went to the other corner; I saw nothing there - as I was putting it down again, I saw a small hole in the straw mattress, I put my hand in, and felt a plate or a dish; I pulled it out - Mr. Phillips claimed it; I put in my hand, and found I believe, seven, which I put into Mr. Phillip's hand, who identified them; John Goodrick took up the plates and dishes, smashed them on the floor, and said "There you b-rs, now can you swear to them;"I called for assistance - John Goodrich got the poker, and said he would smash my b-y brains out; Robert Goodrick then ran up, passed my man on the stairs, and caught up another poker - John said "Come on Bob, let us beat their h-y brains out, if they do not get out of the house;" I got out, and sent for three of my own men - the prisoners were then apprehended, I found a dark lantern and a cutlas, but nothing more relating to this indictment.
Cross-examined. Q. Robert did not come up till there was a row? A. As soon as I called for my assistant, Robert ran up, and passed him; I believe John is married, and has one or two children - these are the broken plates and dishes.
MR. PHILLIPS. I am son of Harry Phillips. I was with Palmer; what he has stated is correct - there was a woman in bed.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there any private marks on these plates and dishes? A. No; I was perfectly aware this was my father's property, when it was taken from the bed.
WILLIAM HALLETT. I was charged to aid and assist Palmer to search the house; what he has stated is correct.
ANN BROWN re-examined. It was a large sized glass; it filled the fire place - this is a fruit-dish that matches with what were taken; I think we lost five dishes, nine or ten plates, and some glasses.
JOHN GOODRICK 's Defence. They say it is a dwelling-house, when I know the house has been locked up for months and months, and not a soul has been seen there, but the woman has come and unlocked it in the morning, to give it air; it is a summer-house, about four hundred yards from the dwelling-house - I have had that dark-lantern for many years; I use it in the boat, to sort my fish at Billingsgate, in the winter.
JOHN GOODRICK - GUILTY. Aged 30.
ROBERT GOODRICK - GUILTY. Aged 21.
Of Stealing only. - Transported for Seven Years. There was another indictment against the prisoners, which was not tried.

Second London Jury. - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
979. ANN HANDS, NICHOLAS BROWN, and BRIDGET LANE were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 36 curb-chains, value 12s.; 73 curb-books, value 3s., and 1 handkerchief, value 6d., the goods of William Benton.
WILLIAM BENTON. I am an ironmonger, and live in a court in Whitecross-street. On the 1st of April I went with these things to a public-house, to speak to a friend, and set my bundle on a cask by the side of the bar; I missed it in a minute; I went to the door, and a person said if any body has taken your bundle it is two women and a man, who are gone along Beech-street; we went along there, into the French Horn public-house, and saw the three prisoners there together; I said, "It is very strange my bundle is gone, and no one has been in the house but yourselves;" Brown and Lane said, "We have no bundle" - Hands said nothing; in about a minute and a half I saw my bundle under Hand's shawl; I said,"You are a bad woman, here is my bundle;" "Here is your bundle," said she.
JOHN AXE. I am a shoemaker. I was in the King's Arms, and saw the three prisoners there; they had a quartern of gin; I did not see the bundle taken - I came out with them, and saw them go on towards the French Horn, in Beech-street; when the bundle was missed, I went in, and said it must have been taken by the two women and the man who had been leaning against the cask; I followed them into the French Horn, and asked if they had taken a bundle by mistake from the Cat, as it is called; they said they had not; but the prosecutor and the officer went in, and Hands gave it up.
JOSEPH ORTON. I was coming by, and went into the public-house; Hands said, "This is your bundle, take it;" I said I should take her into custody - "Brown said,"You have got your bundle, what more do you want;"on going to the Compter, Hands said Brown gave it her; he denied it - he was very much intoxicated.
THOMAS JONES. I am a constable. I was sent for to assist to take the prisoners; Hands said that Brown took took the bundle, and brought it to the door; Brown said,"O you wicked woman, I never did such a thing.
Hands put in a written defence, stating that Brown had given her the bundle to carry.
HANDS - GUILTY. Aged 36.
Confined One Month.
BROWN - NOT GUILTY.
LANE - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-305]
984. WILLIAM PEACH was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 2 segar-boxes, value 6d., and 5 ozs. weight of segars, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Francis Francis.
FRANCIS FRANCIS. I am a tobacconist. On the 5th of March I went out, at half-past seven o'clock in the morning, and missed these segars, at a quarter before eleven, from my shop-window; I know nothing of the prisoner; these boxes have my writing on them.
JOHN CARLILE. I saw the prisoner, and two others, on the 5th of March, about ten o'clock in the morning - the prisoner had one of these boxes, with a blue apron over it, and one of the others had one; they came down Aldersgate-street, and went up Jewin-crescent; I saw them taking some out of the boxes, and putting them into their pockets - I went up, and they all ran off; I took the prisoner; I picked up this one segar at the corner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along St. Martin's-le-grand, and a man asked me to buy two boxes; I said, No; he then asked me if I kept white mice; I said, No, I kept nothing of the kind; but I bought the boxes for 6d., for my brother to keep silk in.
JOHN CARLILE re-examined. Q. Did he say this at the time? A. No; he threw down his box, and so did the other - the prisoner was very violent indeed; he kicked, tore, and fought me.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Whipped and Discharged.

[trialAccount t18290409-307]
986. CAROLINE JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 kettle, value 10s., and 1 flat-iron, value 6d., the goods of William Carryer.
JAMES MONKS. I am a bricklayer. I was at work in a passage near Red Lion-market, on the 19th of March; I saw the prisoner come down the passage without any thing - she walked up the passage again, to the yard-door of the house, and went into the yard; I was about a dozen yards from her; I got down, and looked at her in the yard; I went up again to my work, and for about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes I watched the place, but no one came; I then sent the labourer to see if any one was in the water-closet - he said there was not, but the flapdoor was open; I said it had been shut before, I went, and the flap was up; in about half an hour I saw the prisoner coming out - I knew she was the person who went in; the wind nearly blew her bonnet off, and she put her hands to keep it on - she asked the way to Bunhill-row, and we directed her; I then saw she had a bundle in her left-hand; I called her, but she did not come - I sent the labourer to bring her back - we inquired for the prosecutor; his servant came, and we found on the prisoner this kettle and flat-iron.
Prisoner. Q. Was I not intoxicated? A. She was rather tipsy - this was between two and three o'clock in the afternoon.
EUPHEMIA NESS. I am servant to Mr. William Carryer. Monks called me - I found the prisoner; I asked what she had got; she said, "Nothing of ours;" I said, "Let me look under your cloak," and found this kettle and flat-iron; the kettle had been taken from under the dresser in the front-kitchen, and the iron from the mantel-piece.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 33.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Seven Days.
[trialAccount t18290409-308]
987. HUGH ELLIS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 pair of shoes, value 4s., and 1 pair of boots, value 4s., the goods of James Smetzer, his master.
JAMES SMETZER. I am a shoemaker. I have employed the prisoner occasionally; I employed him on the 28th of March; he left at near twelve o'clock at night. On Wednesday, the 1st of April, I missed a pair of shoes out of a lot of sixty-nine pairs, which I had had in on the Monday night - I did not miss my boots, but I have seen them and the shoes since, and can swear to them.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. The shoes, you say. you think are part of a lot that came in on the Monday? A. This pair of shoes are not one of the sixty-nine pair -I could not state these were on my premises on the Saturday; the prisoner had eight or nine shillings a week, and sometimes as little as six; I always considered him honest - there is a mark on the shoes.
STEPHEN STEVENS. I am a pawnbroker. I have a pair of shoes and a pair of boots pawned on the 28th of March by the prisoner.
JOHN CURTIS. I took the prisoner, and found this duplicate on him, which relates to these other shoes which were found at another pawnbroker's.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18290409-309]
988. HENRY RAFFIN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 5 pairs of shoes, value 9s. 6d., and I pair of boots, value 16s., the goods of Joseph Preston, his master.
JOSEPH PRESTON. I keep a shop in Seacoal-lane -The prisoner was in my employ on the 1st of March, and on the 3d of March I missed my boots and shoes, which were safe in my shop about twelve o'clock on the 2d of March; I was in the habit of taking my goods home at night, and when I went that night I could not get admittance - the next morning I sent my lad for the goods; he came back, and gave me information - I went, and we could not get in; I went with an officer in the evening, about ten o'clock - the property was gone; we raised a ladder, and found the prisoner in a top room.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. How long has the shop been opened? A. I think about a fortnight; it had been empty, but I do not know that loose persons went to sleep there - the prisoner used to sleep there; I had his direction from his brother, and had him taken into custody - I live in Moor-lane.
CHARLES SENIOR. I am a boot and shoemaker. On the 2d or 3d of March, a man came into my shop with five pairs of mended-up shoes, and asked my 6s. for them - I gave him 5s.; on the 5th of March the prosecutor came and claimed one pair of them; I bought them of a man a good deal like the prisoner, but cannot swear it was him.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q Are you in the habit of buying of many persons who hawk shoes about? A. Yes.
JAMES BARNET. On the 2d of March, in the evening, I bought this pair of boots, but I do not know of whom - it was a person I never saw before; I do not think the prisoner is the person - I said so before the Magistrate, because the person - I bought them of was very dirty; he was about the prisoner's size - I do not think he is the man.
ANTHONY REPTON. I took the prisoner in Maidenhead-court; I received these boots from Barnet, who said he bought them of the prisoner - the prisoner made no answer.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Transported for Seven Years.

990. CHARLES COX was indicted for a misdemeanor.
ANN HAZEL. I am the wife of James Hazel - we live in Jewin-street. On the 24th of February the prisoner came to our shop, and offered what appeared to be a half-sovereign, in payment for a milliner's doll, which came to 2s. 6d; I gave him 7s. 6d. change - I put the half-sovereign into my till; there was no other money there - I afterwards gave it to Mr. Harris.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you sure there was no other money in the till? A. Yes - I gave the change from my pocket: I did not know him before- I am not mistaken in his person.
JAMES HARRIS. I was in the room adjoining the shop - Hazell showed me this coin; I pursued, and took the prisoner, who had gone through some courts leading to Whitecross-street - he was walking; I said we wanted him, he had been passing a bad half-sovereign - he said,"If it is a had one, I took it, and don't want any brother about it, I will give you another;" I said, "We think you knew it was a bad one" - we took him into a public-house, where the officer took him.
DANIEL USALL. I am a haberdasher, and live in Aldersgate-street. On the 24th of February, between twelve and one o'clock, the prisoner came to look at a pair of gloves; he selected a pair, and threw down what appeared to be a half-sovereign - I said we had not change; he said, "I will call again in a few minutes, if you will wrap them up, and get me a riding-belt in the mean time" - I followed him, and watched him into Hazel's shop; there was another person with him.
WILLIAM BETTERTON. I took the prisoner, and produce this half-sovereign, as it appears to be.
JOHN FIELD. I am inspector of counterfeit coin - this is a gilt sixpence.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-312]
991. JOHN WILKINSON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-313]
992. WILLIAM PERRY was indicted for a misdemeanor.
DANIEL THORN. I am in the service of Mr. Edward Pontifex and others; they are coppersmith s, and live in Shoe-lane. On the 14th of March the prisoner came and brought this order, which he said he brought from James Walker - (read)
Order from J. Walker Harp-alley. 14th March, 1829. No. 876.
Please to let the bearer have, or send as soon as possible, two copper tea-kettles, one six quarts, and one four quarts.
W. SPARKES.
Mr. William Sparkes is foreman to Mr. Walker - this is very similar to his hand-writing; I let the prisoner have the kettles.
THOMAS JAMES FRANCIS. I was in the public-house adjoining Worship-street office; the prisoner was brought in there - he sat down, and a man said to him,
"I am sorry to see you in such a situation;" the officer put some papers before him - one was an order for two tea-kettles, and one for some lead; the prisoner said he was not certain whether he signed the order for the two tea-kettles, but the one for four he did not sign - he said he had been drinking three or four days, and I believe he said he was stone blind drunk; he seemed absorbed in grief.
CHARLES JAMES WALKER. I took the prisoner, and found these orders on him - one is for two tea-kettles, and one for lead; I did not hear the conversation just stated.
JOHN SHEPHERD. I searched the prisoner, and found these orders in his possession, and this bill for four kettles, which were got from Mr. Pontifex.
JAMES WALKER. I am the employer of William Sparks. The prisoner has been in my employ - I discharged him five weeks ago; I did not give him any anthority to get these things; this order is not william Sparkes ' writing - the orders were torn from my printed order-book, and are dated the day he left.
Prisoner's Defence. These orders were found in my hat - how I came by them I do not know; I was tipsy all day.
WILLIAM SPARKES. This is not my writing - I never gave it to the prisoner; I never gave him any authority to write my name there.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18290409-314]
993. GEORGE FREDERICK BROWN was indicted for a misdemeanour.
MR. BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
JAMES CAYHILL. I am shopman to Mr. Watson - he is a linen-draper, and lives at No. 55, Holborn-hill. The prisoner was an apprentice to Mr. Trevethan, of Burton-cres -cent; his master had been a customer to us for some time: the prisoner had often been sent, and was well known to us. On the 2d of January he brought some patterns, and said he wanted one pieces of furniture of one pattern, and two of the other; one was tea and drab, and the other tea and red - I served him, and put up a remnant of tea and red with them; they all came to 6l. 15s. 10d.: this is the invoice - he went away with the goods.
ROBERT DUKE. I found these invoices at the prisoner's mother's lodging, and a number of patterns of furniture prints.
JAMES CAYHILL re-examined. On the 16th of January he came again, and had one piece of tea and red striped, two pieces of tea and green, which with the glazing made thewhole bill 6l. 10s. 8d. - this in the invoice. On the 6th of February he came again, and had one piece of tea and drab striped, the same as at first; he had one whole piece, and a large remnant of fifty-one yards of another pattern of ground chintz - they came to 7l. 9s. 9d.; this is the invoice. On the 27th of February he came again, and had one piece of light ground chintz, and said as there was not sufficient of the tea ground chintz, the light ground might he used instead; I put in that parcel a tremnant of forty-nine yards and a half - the bill was 9l. 17s. 2d.
WILLIAM TYLER. I produce this invoice, and this note.
JAMES CAYHILL. I gave the prisoner this note, and this is the invoice of the last article.
Cross-examined by MR. CRESWELL. Q. Had you not instructions to deliver no goods without a written order? A. No; it is most frequently the case that young men produce a written order - not always.
SAMUEL TREVETHAN. I am an upholsterer. The prisoner was my apprentice; I never gave him authority to get these goods, and never had them.
JOHN MOYLE. I am shopman to Mr. Trevethan. I frequently give orders, but never gave the prisoner these orders, and never received the goods.
RICHARD LANGSTAFF. I am in the service of Mr. Trevethan. I never gave the prisoner orders to get these goods.
Prisoner. Q. What goods did you send me for, for a job of your own at Peckham? A. I never did send for any.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18290409-315]
Second London Jury - before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
994. ANN THEW was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 1 cloak, value 7s.; 1 gown, value 1s, 6d., and 1 pair of shoes, value 1s. 6d., the goods of William Martin, her master.
WILLIAM MARTIN. I am a schoolmaster, and live in Bull and Mouth-street, The prisoner was in my service, about two months previous to the 13th of February, on which day she left me; we missed the articles stated, and found her about a fortnight afterwards, in Field-lane; the articles were found on her, except the shoes, which were in pawn.
JOSEPH WILLIAMS. I am a constable. I went with the prosecutor to a house on Saffron-hill, where the prisoner was seen to go in; I inquired if there was a person of her name there - they said there was; I met her on the stairs, and asked her if her name was Ann Thew - she said No; I said I would not let her go till I got a light, as it was very dark: I found these articles in the room, not on her; I was told she lodged there - she said she had torn the lining out of the cloak, and threw it in the fire, and the gown was up stairs in the room; there was no threat or promise made to her.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Transported for Seven Years.
The prosecutor stated that he had missed other property which the prisoner acknowledged she had taken.
[trialAccount t18290409-316]
Second Middlesex Jury.
995. GEORGE FRANKLIN, RICHARD HEMMINGS, and JOHN GRAY, were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Atkins, on the 29th of March, and stealing therein 2 pecks of onions, value 2s.; 2 casks, value 3s.; 4 gallons of shrub, value 30s.; 2 gallons of peppermint, value 1l.; 2 gallons of gin, value 18s.; 2 gallons of cloves, value 1l.; 3 gallons of Rum, value 30s., and 2 bottles, value 4d., the goods of the said Thomas Atkins .
MR. BARRY conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS ATKINS. I keep the White Lion public-house, Dancer's-hill, South Mims . My house was safe on the night of the 29th of March, and the cellars quite secure - the next morning, which was Sunday, I went to draw some ale - I saw the cellar broken open; the boards had been broken exactly where the bolts were, and they had put their arm in and drawn the bolts back; I can swear that I missed about ten gallons from different casks, of cloves, peppermint, shrub, rum, and gin - some ale, onions, casks, and stone bottles - one bottle had a little shrub in it, one a little ale, and one was empty; I sent off to Barnet for an officer, and we both examined the premises; a person called on me, and I went with my ostler, cooper, to the field, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon; I found five men drinking in the field, four of whom I knew - the prisoners were three of them; they were drinking ale out of a stonebottle - they all drank in their turns - it was such a bottle as I had lost; Franklin was sitting on one of the casks which I knew - I ordered my ostler to take three of the casks up, and Gray said, "Master took it out;" I said they were my casks - they said if they were mine I might take them; Franklin said they found them in the hedge - I got on the coach to go to Barnet, leaving them with one bottle, pouring ale out; Cooper took the casks to my house - I got two officers, went to South Mims, and found Gray in a chandler's-shop; we went to a cottage near where they had been drinking and found Franklin - my ostler and I then got on a coach, and took Hemmings about a mile on this side of St. Alban's.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. This breaking open of your cellar took place on the night of the 29th of March? A. Yes; or early on the following morning; I went to bed about eleven o'clock, and was the last person up - the cellar is in the garden - it is not under the same roof as the house - there is a side door from the kitchen into it; it was open day-light when these men were sitting drinking - Gray asked me to pour some out for them - they were all pretty far gone; two or three of them seemed as if they were able to understand me; I believe the prisonerswere so tipsy that they did not know what they were about; there is a footpath near the place where they were, but they are bye fields - I went back to the spot where they had been, and brought away the other bottle; there was nothing to prevent our taking them - but I did not know I might without an officer.
JOHN COOPER. I am the ostler. I went to the field in the afternoon, and found the three prisoners and the other men as my master has stated.
FREDERICK PROPSTEING. I was applied to on the morning of the 29th, went in the evening to the chandler's-shop, and took Gray in South Mims; I kept him - and the others went and brought Franklin.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-317]
OLD COURT.
EIGHTH DAY. SATURDAY, APRIL 18.
Third Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Recorder.
996. JOHN PETTET, JAMES WHITE, JAMES MARTIN, and SAMUEL HILLICK were indicted forfeloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Sarah Howorth, on the 3d of April, at St. Luke, and stealing therein 2 tables, value 10s.; 4 chairs, value 10s., and 2 candlesticks, value 3s., the goods of Sarah Howorth; I trunk, value 10s., and 10 sovereigns, the property of Joseph Bennet; 2 chests of drawers, value 16s.; 2 coats, value 7s.; one pair of trousers, value 4s., and 40 fishing-lines, value 10s., the goods of William Gardner and another.
MESSERS. BOLLAND & CRESWELL conducted the prosecution.
SARAH HOWORTH. I am single, and live at No. 42, Goswell-street, in the parish of St. Luke; I have lived there thirty-five years - Mr. Robert Ray is the landlord, and has been so about twenty-one years. On the 3d of April I was at home, and in consequence of information I saw Pettet about half-past one o'clock in the passage of My house; he demanded 120l. for six years' back rent - I told him I did not owe any such money; I asked him to walk up stairs - he followed me up, accompanied by two more men, who I cannot identify; Pettet pulled out a paper, and read it - I do not know what it was; he then demanded the rent in the name of William Chapman - I should not know the paper again, for I did not have it; I never heard of William Chapman in my life - Pettet said his own name was John Smith; he asked me to open my lodger's door, whose name is Bennett - I told him I could not, for my lodger was out; his door was fastened with a padlock outside - I was very much alarmed; he told me not to flurry myself, for I should have five days to replevie if he was in the wrong - when I said I could not open Bennett's door, he said he could not wait a minute; I said, "I have another way backwards, if you will wait while I go round and see if that is open;" he said he should not wait a minute - he rushed by me, and wrenched the padlock off before my face; he went into the room, and the two men with him - I did not go into the room; I went down-stairs, and went out to look for my brother - I was absent nearly a quarter of an hour; when I came back there was a van at the door loaded with goods - I thought I saw a table on it which was mine; I did not notice any thing else - when I got into the house it was nearly empty, and the house was so full of people in the passage I could hardly get in; I found Mr. Butler there, who is the rent-gatherer for Mr. Ray - he has been so about ten or eleven years; I never knew any other rent - gather during that time - I had some conversation with Butler, and then fetched Hawthorne, the officer: he came back with me - Pettet was then standing at the post of the parloar-door, and I gave a charge of him - the constable asked him by what authority he took the goods; he refused to show his authority; he said he was not going; to have it turn up - two tables were taken out of the house, four chair, two brass candlesticks, one pair of cotton stockings, and some crockery; the house was nearly empty - I had never received any notice not to pay the rent to Butler.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Since you have been tenant of these premises has any person demanded rent from you, except Mr. Ray and Butler? A. Not since Mr. Ray has had the house; before that Mr. Barker did - I have not paid any rent for four years, because I have a little property which I cannot get at, and Mr. Ray is so kind as to wait for it; I am confident Pettet did not say he had come as an agent of Smith or by his authority - he said his own name was Smith; I was very much alarmed, but I am certain of that; he demanded the rent in the name of Chapman, and said his own name was Smith: he did not represent that he was acting for Smith; I first saw him in the passage; I think the door was shut when I saw him; the passage was full of people, when he refused to show his authority for fear it should be torn up - he did not say I should have a list of the articles taken away; he was asked for the inventory, and said he had got it, but would not show it.
MR. CRESWELL. Q. Who demanded to see the distresswarrant? A. Butler and the constable.
WILLIAM GARDNER. I lodge at Miss Howorth's. I remember the Friday that these things were taken out of the house; I saw Pettet that day - there was a knock at the door, it was opened by a laid inside, and Pettet came in; he came along the passage to my door, and asked me for Sarah Howorth - I said if he went down the passage and knocked at the door there, he would find her; he went away as if to look for her; he returned and came into my room, and said he wanted 120l. for six years back-rent; I said I had not got it - I am a fishing-tackle-maker; he produced a paper, but I did not hear him say any thing about it; he sat down in a chair, began to write on the paper, and looked all round about the room; and he had scarcely done writing before some things were taken out of the place; a man in top-boots came and moved the things out - they took a chest of drawers and other things, what I cannot say, for I left the room at the time - the man in top-boots is not in custody; I am certain of Pettet's person - I left the room; I saw another chest of drawers taken from my room - they were taken to a van at the door; Martin was in the van - I shortly afterwards found Mr. Butler in the passage, in conversation with Pettet; Butler asked him who authorised him to make the seizure; he said a person named Chapman; I heard no christian name - Butler asked him his own name, and he said it was Smith; Butler demanded to see the warrant - Pettet said he had shown it to so many he was afraid of its being torn; Miss Howorth thengave him into custody; I do not remember any of the other prisoners being there; I had some stuffed birds in my room - Pettet packed up an owl which was in a case into a hamper. and several other smaller stuffed birds; my two chests of drawers were worth 10s.; I had two coats in them worth 7s., a pair of trousers worth 4s., five bundles of string worth 2s. 6d.; my brother is a birdstuffer - there were some artificial birds' eyes to the value of 3l. taken; I lost three dozen and half of fishing-lines, but, cannot say he took them; I lost a case with three birds, with three divisions, worth 18s., and three fishing-rods - I remember White standing in the passage.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When it was discovered to be an improper seizure - a good many things were put back into the house? A. Yes; I was not present then - I did not know Martin before; he was not taken into custody - he went to Worship street of his own accord, and attended twice.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Do you know Petit? A. Yes; I had not seen him before; a little boy let him in at the street door - he told Butler he had shewn his authority to so many, he was afraid it would he torn up; I think the whole of the things were not put into the van at the time he was taken into custody.
Q. Did he make the least resistance? A. Yes; he would not go to the office for some time; he did not say he had shown his authority to the proper person, and they had no right to take him.
Prisoner WHITE. Q. Was I at all violent in my behaviour in the house? A. No; I only saw you standing still in the passage.
MARY ANN LOADER. I am single, and live with Miss Howorth. I was at home on the 3d of April; she was called out of the room I was in, about half-past one; and while she was out I heard a noise on the stairs - I went into the passage to see what it was, and saw a man in top boots, and Pettet and White on the stairs; while I was in the passage I heard a noise of a padlock breaking, and saw Miss Howorth come down; I saw Petit a very few minutes afterwards - he came into my room - I told him I hoped he had not come to strip us of all our things; he said, I might take a few things out, if I could get them out, but I must be very quick in doing it, for he had got a van, and his men coming directly to clear the house; I lodge in the same room as Miss Howorth - I took some things out, into a court by the side of the house, and then he bolted me out; I was let in again by Mrs. Mackey, who had been at the house for a few nights: when I got in I went into my own room, and into Gardner's room - that was nearly empty - and the chairs, tables, and candlesticks, were taken out of Miss Howorth's room; I saw the same things brought back again; I afterwards went to Bennet's room, and saw Butler there - Petter was there- I did not notice any thing else: I heard Butler ask Pettet for his authority, and he refused to shew it him: Butler asked me, in Petter's hearing, who had put in the seizure? I said Smith, by which name Pettet had appeared) had put it in, in the name of one Chapman - I think he had said William Chapman; Petter was desired to cease- he was stopped with a table, and he insisted on taking it out, saying he was acting under the authority of the King: Hawthorne was sent for - he came, and took Pettet into custody; I saw Pettet take some chairs and tables out of Miss Howorth's room - and he took some candlesticks out of my hand; the things in the room were her's, but I had the use of them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Who brought the things in? A. I brought some in - some persons at the door took them off the van - some were in the passage.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Did not Pettet shew an authority at the time this took place? A. I saw him show some sort of paper at one time to some of the persons, but what it was I do not know - he afterwards refused to shew it to Butler.
Q. Did he not say you might take some things, if you could get them out before some persons came to the house? A. If I could do it before the van came: I never heard him say he was agent to Smith; the things were brought back after he was in custody; some of the other lodgers' things were also brought back - but not till after he was in custody.
JOHN PATTEN. I am a fishing-tackle maker, and lodge at Miss Howorth's, on the ground-floor. About half-past one o'clock on this day I was in Miss Howorth's room, and heard somebody call out Miss Howorth - I opened her room door, and saw Pettet and White, and a man in top boots, standing in the passage; I said, "Miss Howorth, you are wanted," and she went out - in about five minutes I went into Gardner's room; I found nobody there but Gardner and his brother - Pettet came into the room; I asked him who authorised him to come and make a seizure in that room; he told me to mind my own business, or else he would charge a constable with me, and said he was acting under the King - he said he wanted 120l. for six years' back-rent; I said, "This young man don't owe any rent, he has got three or four weeks good yet" - he told me again to mind my own business, or else he would charge a constable with me; I said, "You won't be so hard - this poor young man has got but one hand, let him have a thing or two that he gets his living by." I asked him for his nets, his fishing canns, and gun; he said, be quick, for directly the van came he should clear the house immediately - I went up stairs into Miss Howorth's bed-room, and got two or three small things out, and when I came down the van was at the door, partly loaded - Martin was in the van; I saw Hillick bringing a trunk down stairs; he took it to the van - Pettet at that time was giving the men directions to be quick; and I saw Pettet with a table in the passage just before Butler came - it had been taken from Miss Howorth's room; after this Pettet was taken into custody - I afterwards assisted in unloading the van; Watts, the street-keeper, was there - I was in the van; Martin asked who authorised me to move the goods from the van - Watts showed his staff and said, "I authorised him." Martin said,"Where am I to get my money from?" I said, "You must look to those who employed you." Martin assisted me in moving the heaviest things directly Watts showed, his staff.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. He was the owner of the van? A. I believe so; he gave assistance when he found things were not right, and so did all the prisoners except Petit, but he was not there - Hillicksaid he was glad it had turned out as it did, and that the things had not been taken away, when he found it was an illegal seizure, and so did White; they all appeared to be acting under Pettet's order.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Was Petit given into custody - before or after the seizure was completed? A. The van was very nearly loaded when he was taken; whether any thing was put into it after he was taken I cannot say - it was never wholly filled; Pettet said he had proper authority, and if I interrupted him, he should give me into custody - I saw nothing put into the van after he was taken.
JOSEPH BENNET. I am a mail-coachman, and lodge in the first floor of this house. On Friday, the 3d of April, I went out about ten o'clock in the morning; I padlocked my door - I returned about six in the evening, and found my room door broken open, and some of my things in the passage; there was a large portmanteau trunk, and part of my bedstead - I had left my keys in my trunks, and they had locked the trunks and took the keys with them; my bedstead was all in pieces, and had not been unscrewed, but strained out from one to the other, and pulled to pieces - my keys were returned to me next morning; on going into my room I found all my things thrown about; I had a table in the room with a drawer in it, and I had put ten sovereigns and twenty shillings in that drawer that morning - I did not lock the drawer; the money was there when I left the room, and when I returned it was gone - I missed several articles out of the room; two glasses and other things; the bedding was all tied up.
Cross-examined. Q. You have no means of ascertaining who took the money? A. No.
THOMAS BUTLER. I live at No. 92, Mary-street, Hampstead-road; I am employed by Mr. Ray to collect his rents, and have been employed so about ten years - among others I collected from Miss Howorth; I abstained, by direction of Mr. Ray, from pressing her for rent - she owed between four and five years' rent; on Friday, the 3d of April, I went to her house, and saw a great number of persons at the door, and also a van partly laden with household furniture, as it appeared to me - I went into the house, and saw Pettet and Hillick nearly at the bottom of the stairs; Hillick was carrying a table, and Pettet behind him - I insisted on their not taking it out of the house -I stopped it, as the passage was narrow, and desired them not to remove any thing out of the house; Hillick stopped directly, and Pettet said, if any body interrupted him in what he was doing, he would send for a constable and give them in charge - I asked him to show me his authority for making the distress; he told me he should not show it me at all - I insisted on seeing it, and then we went into a back room; he produced a paper, which he held with both his hands, leaving only a part of it to be seen - I read part of it, and asked him who put in the distress, and he said William Chapman, whose name was on the paper; I asked him his name, and he said it was John Smith, and I saw that on the paper also - this is the paper (looking at it); in consequence of this, I sent for Mr. Minshull, clerk to Messrs. Freshfield, who are Mr. Ray's solicitors - I saw White and Martin at the house; White was just at the entrance of the garden door - I did not see him do any thing; he said he was employed by Smith - mentioning the name of Smith; a constable was sent for.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. He held the authority in both hands, did you get it into your hands? A. Never. I have collected the rent about ten years; I believe a claim has been set up to the premises by a person of the name of Young; nobody else that I know of- there is, I believe, an action in the Court of King's Bench which is not yet decided.
MR. BOLLAND. Q. The action is by Mr. Young? A. Yes. I never heard the name of Chapman or Pettet; I have received the rent for all these houses ever since I have collected for Mr. Ray.
MR. LEE. Q. Would you necessarily be in possession of the circumstance if any claim was set up? A. It might have been without my knowledge. White and Hillick came to the office twice voluntarily.
HENRY KEIBLE. I am in the employ of William Gardner. On the 3d of April I went to the street-door, as Mr. Gardner sent me; I heard a knock at the door - I opened it, and Pettet. White, and a man in top-boots came in; they came to Mr. Gardner's room door; I saw the men afterwards putting the things into the van - Pettet was taking them out of the house.
JOHN MINSHULL. I am clerk to Messrs. Freshfield and Son, who are solicitors to Mr. Ray. On the 3d of April, being sent for, I went down to this house, and saw a number of people at the door; a van was there, partly laden with goods - I went in, and saw Pettet; I did not see Martin - I told Pettet I came from Messrs, Freshfield and Son, solicitors to Mr. Ray and Mr. Oliver, and requested to know by what authority he distrained; he said, "By authority of Mr. Chapman;" I asked him to produce his authority - he refused, and was taken away in custody; I saw White near Gardner's parlour door - I asked him what he was doing there; he said he was the man in possession - I desired him to go out; he said he would, and he did; I directed the van to be unloaded - I saw Pettet afterwards at a public-house by the Police-office, and asked him where Chapman lived; he said he did not know - I said, "Has he any house in London?" he said he could not tell, he did not know that he had - I asked if he knew where to find him; he said he did not - he believed he had gone to Manchester; I then asked if he could find him at Manchester; he said he could not - I then asked him where the warrant was signed; he said at a public-house in Goswell-street; I then asked him his name and address - he said, "John Smith, North side, Bethnal-green;" I asked him if he was a broker - he said he was; I asked if he kept a shop or a close house - he said it was a house, he kept no shop; I afterwards attended before the Magistrate - (looking at some papers) - these are what the prisoner put in before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Hillick was bailed before the Magistrate? A. Yes, and has surrendered to-day; I had no opportunity of seeing who loaded and unloaded the van.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Are you sure Pettet did not say he acted as agent for Smith, who lived in Bethnal-green? A. No, he did not; I am quite positive; he said he met him at a public-house in Goswell-street - not at the Horns, in Smithfield.
WILLIAM HERETAGE. I am clerk at Worship-street; when the prisoner was brought before the Magistrate, he was asked his name, and he gave it John Smith - these papers are what he produced before the Magistrate.
The documents were here read; the first was a printed distress warrant, authorising John Smith to seize the goods on the premises, No. 42, Goswell-street, in the possession of Mrs. Howorth, for £120. signed William Chapman, of the said premises, and dated the 1st of April, 1829. The other was a printed form of inventory; the articles enumerated in it were in the following words:- "Bedsteads, chairs, tables, sundry boxes, glass, sundry stuffed birds, dressers, benches, boxes, stores, and all other goods, chattels, and effects about the premises," and signed"John Smith, No. 26, North side Bethnal-green."
THOMAS HAWTHORNE. I am a headborough. On the 3d of April I went to Howorth's house, as she came for me; I saw a crowd at the door, and the van partly loaded -I saw Martin standing by the van, and Hillick outside the door; as I went into the passage I saw Pettet - he said,"Are you an officer?" I said I was; he said, "If you know your duty, I know mine:" I asked him by what authority he was there; he said he had authority to seize for 120l. due for six years rent, and asked me to go up to the first floor, occupied by Bennett. which I did; and he showed me a paper which he held in both his hands - I saw the name of Smith to it; I asked his name, and he said John Smith - he said that at the house; and at the Police-office, after the examination, he told me he lived at the house of Mr. Jackson, North side Bethnal-green, opposite the Greyhound public-house; I found No. 26 on the inventory - I went to Bethnal-green, and made every inquiry I could, but found no such person; the North side of Bethnal-green only consists of twelve houses- the Greyhound is not opposite the North side, nor nearly opposite - it is five hundred yards from the North side; I inquired, but could find no No. 26 in the whole neighbourhood.
WILLIAM GARDNER. I saw Pettet writing the inventory; he left none on the premises, to my knowledge.
EDWIN SALVIN. I am landlord of the Dundee Arms public-house, Bethnal-green, and have lived there two years; there is no such number as 26 on the North side of the green; I believe the highest number is 12 - I know no such name as Pettet living there; I know no Mr. Jackson on the North side.
GEORGE PETTINGER. I live at the Bear and Staff public-house, Smithfield. I have known Pettet a long while- he is a jobbing carpenter. On the 3d of April he was sleeping at my house, and had for some time; whenever he worked at this end of the town he slept at our house- that was his general abode; I knew him by the name of Pettet - I never knew him pass by the name of John Smith.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. How many years have you known him? A. Between two and three; he built me some houses at Bethnal-green; I never heard any thing bad of him - he was a very steady good workman, and I thought him honest.
COURT. Q. Did you ever know him as a broker? A. He has been one, I believe, but do not know it; I never knew him act as one, nor am I aware of his acting as one at Manchester.
Prisoner PETTET. I leave my defence to my Counsel.
PETTET - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 49.
WHITE - NOT GUILTY.
MARTIN - NOT GUILTY.
HILLICK - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-318]
996. THOMAS WHITE was indicted for feloniously forging a certain order, with intent to defraud Alexander Archibald .
SECOND COUNT, for uttering the same knowing it to be forged.
ANN FIFIELD. I kept an eating-house in Crown-court, Temple-bar. Mr. Archibald discounted a bill for 21l. 13s. 9d. for me on the 14th of March; I was to take two dozen of sherry wine, at 40s. a dozen, in part of the cash: it was to have been delivered when I sent for it - I knew the prisoner for nine or ten weeks; I never authorized him to apply for the wine: I knew nothing of this till the 1st of April, when I sent to Archibald the billbroker for the wine (looking at the order); I never signed this, nor authorized any body to put my name to it.
ALEXANDER ARCHIBALD. I am an accountant, and live at No. 8. Thayer-street, Manchester-square. I am in the habit of discounting bills; I was not acquainted with Fifield - I discounted this bill for her; one of my clients did it, Mr. John Whitaker - he is a gentleman, he is not a wine-merchant: the bill had three months to run; I gave her 16l. in cash - she was to have two dozens of wine, at 40s. a dozen, and 2s. 6d. a dozen for the bottles; my commission and postage was 1l. 3s. 6d. - I wrote four or five letters before it was settled, and the interest was 5s. 3d.; the letters were by the post: they were 1s. 6d. - Whitaker lives in Essex, at Colchester; I do not know in what street; my clients come and say if I can get good bills they will advance money; Mr. Whitaker was to send the wine; it was left at my house - I do not know where it came from: I do not know the value of the wine, for I never tasted it -I was told it was sherry; when I came home my servants told me two dozens were left for Fifield; I had no bill of parcel of it; I sent it from my house to the prisoner's, in consequence of this order which he brought on or about the 17th of March - I did not know him till he brought Mrs. Fifield to me to have the bill discounted; I saw her endorse the bill.
JURY. Q. How did you receive the money for the bill? A. I received instruction to pay the money by letter- I held money of his; I have not brought his letters here- they are in existence - (order read)
Blackfriars. 17th of March.
MR. ARCHIBALD, SIR, - Give the bearer, Mr. White, wine to the amount of balance due to me. I trust it will be good; Mr. White is to sell it for me. Yours, ANN FIFIELD.
JOSEPH HURREN. I am clerk to Mr. Archibald. I took the two dozens of wine to the prisoner's house; one dozen at a time - I delivered them at his lodgings No. 37, Hadlow-street, Burton-crescent; I do not know how the wine came to Mr. Archibald - I only know it was the colour of Sherry.
ALEXANDER ARCHIBALD re-examined. Mr. Whitaker was to send the wine to my house, and I found it there; I do not know where it came from - he ordered a person in London to send it; it stood in my hall in bottles, not in a hamper.
Prisoner. Q. When the 16l. was paid to Mrs. Fifield, did not you say you would give the balance, 5l. 13s. 9d. in wine, equal to that at 40s. a dozen from any winemerchant in London? A. No; I said the gentleman proposed to give two dozens at 40s.; I have no doubt it was worth that - I undertook that it should he; I have no doubt it was so from the respectability of my client - Mrs. Fifield endorsed the bill in my drawing-room; the bill is not in my possession; I do not know where it is.
MRS. FIFIELD. The prisoner introduced me to Archibald - his brother is a baker, and had lived in Temple-street; I owed him 17s. 6d. and he called for it - the prisoner was with him, and this is the way I first knew him; he offered to sell me whiskey and different spirits; I owed a quarter's rent, and had a man in possession - the prisoner said if I had a good bill he could get it discounted, and introduced me to Archibald.
Prisoner, Q. Did you endorse the bill in Archibald's presence? A. I endorsed it, but not in his presence - I did it at my brother's, in Red Lion-street, Holborn: Archibald was not there - my brother drew the bill by my authority, but I endorsed it; my brother is the acceptor - he was in my debt.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner apply to you about the wine? A. Never; I intended to dispose of it, and had a friend who would have taken it.
The prisoner, in his Defence, entered in'o a long account of his first acquaintance with the prosecutrix, and stated that she had desired him to dispose of the wine, and promised to give him 3l. for his trouble in introducing her to Archibald. That after she had received the money they went to a public-house in the Strand, where he wrote the order in question which the prosecutrix did not sign, (not being able to write.)
ANN FIFIELD. I swear I never gave him reason to suppose I intended to dispose of the wine, or to give him any thing for getting the bill discounted.
HENRY SANDERS. Mrs. Fifield is my sister - I kept an eating-house in Red Lion-street. I signed her name as the drawer of the bill, as she was not competent at the moment; I also accepted it as drawn on me; I know she can write - I believe I endorsed the bill, but am not certain; she was to have two dozen of wine if I or White could find a customer - he was to sell it for her; this is what I heard from White afterwards; I never heard from her that she had any idea of either of us disposing of it; when the bill was drawn, I told her I would prevail on some friends to buy some - and I intimated to White that I should be glad if he could dispose of it.
JOSEPH HURREN. When I took the wine I saw the prisoner; he asked why Mr. Archibald did not send another dozen of wine - I said I was only ordered to bring two; he said, "Tell him I will stand none of his nonsense - if he does not send another dozen he shall feel the consequence of it."
MRS. FIFIELD. The prisoner never asked me to sign the order; nor did I know he had written it till after the wine was gone. NOT GUILTY.
