NEW COURT, Eighth Day.
Fifth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
900. RICHARD HARPER GREEN was indicted for that he, on the 5th of September, at St. Mary Matfellon, alias Whitechapel, having in his custody and possession, a certain bill of exchange, which is as follows: 20l. 0s. 0d. London, September 5, 1828.
Three months after date, pay to my order Twenty Pounds, value received. R.H. GREEN.
Mr. James Busby, No. 35, King-street, Goswell-street, London, feloniously did falsely make, forge, and counterfeit upon the said bill of exchange an acceptance thereof, with intent to defraud John Hipkiss; against the Statute, & c.
SECOND COUNT, for uttering the same with a like intent knowing it to be forged.
JOHN HIPKISS. I am a nail-manufacturer, and live at Dudley, in Worcestershire. The prisoner was a pretended agent, to sell goods for people - I have known him several years, and have had dealings with him in business; a little more than two years ago, he applied to me to sell nails for me; he did so, and went on for about twelve months, and he got the account in such a confused state, that I came up to London myself, about November, 1827- he could not give any satisfactory account of what he had been doing, and I was not satisfied; I appointed another agent, Mr. William Harris, the prisoner after that had goods regularly from Harris; I was in the country, but on some complaint from Mr. Harris, I came to town in January last; the prisoner came to my inn several times, but nothing particular passed relative to this bill of 20l. - he promised to arrange his account; I do not recollect his saying any thing to me about this bill - there were several other bills.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. You are a nail-manufacturer? A. Yes; I used to send goods to the prisoner's orders; you may say he was my agent if you like - he was at that time; when I was in town he came to my inn two or three times, but he never went with me to the place where the bill was made payable, to see if Mr. Busby was to be found; I went with Mr. Harris to No. 35, King-street, Goswell-street, to make inquiries - the prisoner went with us part of the way, but did not go there; he went within a street of the place: the endorsement on the back of this bill is my hand-writing - it has passed through my hands.
This was a bill for 25l. 7s. 6d., dated the 27th of May, 1828, at three months date; drawn by R. H. Green, on Mr. Busby, and endorsed, "Received Spooner and Co."
WILLIAM HARRIS. I was appointed agent to Mr. Hipkiss, in November, 1827. The prisoner still continued to sell nails for Mr. Hipkiss - he was still a commissioned salesman for Mr. Hipkiss, but I had the bills in my hands, and the list of the stock was left in my hands, with orders to the persons at the wharf not to deliver any nails but to my order - this bill for 20l., and another for 18l. 5s. 6d., I received from the prisoner at the same time; I am not quite positive when; but I am satisfied in my own mind it was on the 5th of September, the day the bills were drawn, or just thereabout; it was for the purpose of paying for nails -I remitted them with three others in a paper parcel to Mr. Hipkiss; when the first became due, which was no the 8th or 9th of December, it was returned - Mr. Hipkiss received notice of it; I heard of it soon afterwards; I wrote to Mr. Hipkiss, and requested him to come to town, and he came very early in January - I saw the prisoner soon after I heard of the bill being dishonoured, and he told me it wouldbe paid in a very few days; I took no steps, but waited for Mr. Hipkiss to come.
Cross-examined. Q. Then the prisoner still continued to be agent for the sale of these nails? A. He still continued to sell nails for Mr. Hipkiss - he did not cease to sell after November, 1827; I saw him very frequently - I have seen him with a person, whom I supposed to be his brothere, but he never told me it was his brother; I met him with him two or three times - I do not know that he was in the habit of assisting him in business; I have never seen him when I have gone on business - I think he used to go out to solicit orders for his brother; I fear he did - I never knew he had a brother till Mr. Hipkiss told me; he had a brother, who was a very indifferent character, and he was fearful of any nails going to him; his name was John; I do not know that he has absconded.
JOHN SWAIN. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and produce the bill, which I received from Mr. Hipkiss, by the order of Mr. Payne, the clerk, at Guildhall; I did not apprehend him about this case.
MR. HIPKISS re-examined. Q. From whom did you receive this bill? A. From my banker's; I always suspected John was at the head of this business, but the prisoner used always to deny it to me: I have reason to believe it was his brother who led him into this - I had a bad opinion of him.
WILLIAM HARRIS re-examined. That is the bill I received in September - it is one of those which I received from the prisoner; I inquired for Mr. Busby at No. 35, King-street, Goswell-street, but I found no such person living there - the bill for 18l. 5s. 6d. was due a month later.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you make this inquiry? A. Some time after the bill was due; I saw a female - I did not know at that time who lived in the house, but I find the name is Williams; I do not know who lodges in the house - bills are frequently made payable where parties do not live; I inquired if there had been any nails delivered there, and they said No.
Bill read, see indictment.
JOHN WILLIAMS. I am a tailor. I lived for four years and a half at No. 35, King-street, Goswell-street; I left there on the 25th of March last - I never knew of a person of the name of Busby living there at any time; I believe there was a bill presented there five or six months ago - I cannot say exactly when.
Prisoner's Defence (written) My Lord, and Gentlemen of the Jury, - I beg leave to assure you that I am entirely innocent of the charge imputed to me by this indictment. The prosecutor, Mr. Hipkiss, and myself, have been long acquainted, and in the habit of doing business together, for a long period. He is an iron-merchant in considerable trade, at Dudley, in Worcestershire, and I am an agent for the sale of ironmongery goods in London, selling for the country manufactures on commission. With regard to the transaction in question, I have only to state that my brother, John Green, Jun. who has been in the habit of obtaining orders for me, and transacting a great part of my business, told me. about the month of July last, that he had received an order for a quantity of nails to the amount of about 38l., from one James Busby, whom he represented was living in King-street, Goswell-street; and having had a similar transaction shortly prior, to the amount of 25l, with the same person, through my brother John, I felt no hesitation in executing this order, and accordingly I proceeded to Mr. Harris, the town agent of the prosecutor, and obtained orders for the delivery of the goods from Snell's and Whitehouse's wharfs, in the City-road, which I delivered over to my brother John, together with two bills of exchange for the amount, which I had drawn at two and three months. according to agreement, and directed him to get them accepted by Mr. James Busby, on handing over to him the orders before mentioned. My brother John subsequently returned these bills to me, bearing the acceptance " James Busby;" and having no suspicion whatever of the genuineness of the signatures, I paid them over to Mr. Harris on account of the prosecutor, Mr, Hipkiss, for the goods in question. On the first of these bills becoming due, I accompanied the prosecutor and Mr. Harris to where it was made payable, and waited outside whilst they proceeded to the house; on coming away from which they said no such person as Busby lived there. Gentlemen, I am sorry to add, that, at this period, my brother John, upon whom I had entirely relied, not only in this transaction, but in others in which he has also deceived me in a similar manner, had absconded, and has been out of the way ever since, so that I could obtain no information of Busby, although I have devoted days and nights in endeavouring to do so, a fact both the prosecutor and Mr. Harris are, I apprehend, fully aware of. On the second bill becoming due, that also was, from the same cause, dishonoured: and not being able to take them up, I was, after the expiration of a considerable period, taken into custody, and committed for trial Had I been conscious of having committed any crime affecting my life, it is manifest that I had ample opportunity to escape if I had thought proper to do so.
PHILIP RAWLINSON. I am a clerk to Mr. Armsted, a notary. I presented this 25l. bill for payment, on the 30th of August, 1828, from Spooner and Co.; it is addressed to Mr. James Busby, No. 35, King-street, Goswell-street - I received an answer by a woman, "Pay on Monday;" it was paid at Spooner's on the Monday.
COURT. Q. Have you any recollect on who gave you the answer? A. No, only that it was a woman; I told my employer, who made the minute on the bill - the bankers had left a note in the morning, where to send the money.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-320]
998. RICHARD HARPER GREEN was again indicted for that he, on the 5th of September, at St. Mary, Matfelon, alias Whitehapel, having in his custody and possession a certain bill of exchange, which is as follows:
£18: 5: 6 London, September 5, 1828.
Four months after date. pay to my order Eighteen Pounds, Five Shillings, and Sixpence. Value received, R. H. GREEN. To Mr. James Busby, 35, King-street, Goswell-street, London, he, on the same day, at the same parish, feloniously did falsely make, forge, and counterfeit, upon the said bill of exchange, an acceptance there of, as follows, " James Busby," with intention to defraud John Hipkiss ; against the Statute, & c.
SECOND COUNT, for feloniously nttering and publishing the same as true, well knowing the same to be false, forged, and counterfeited, with intention to defraud the said John Hipkiss; against the Statute, & c.
JOHN HIPKISS. I received this bill with the other, which I understood were paid by the prisoner, on account of goods delivered to Busby, by his order; this is the order- it is Green's hand-writing, and the signature is Busby's- the goods were delivered to Harris, on the 26th of August.
WILLIAM HARRIS. The goods were delivered to this order - the prisoner produced this order to me.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Are you quite sure goods were delivered to this order? A. The goods were fetched from the wharf - I do not live there; I asked the prisoner if they were to be sent, and he said No, the party would send their own cart to fetch them; he had no hand in taking the goods away, that I know of.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner give you this bill of 18l. 5s. 6d. in part of payment? A. Yes. When Mr. Hipkiss came to London, I saw him frequently on the subject of these bills; I went with Mr. Hipkiss to present this bill for payment - it was refused; I am not quite sure whether we saw the prisoner on the road, or whether he went with us, but I had a bag with some tea in it, which I gave the prisoner to hold, and he waited in Compton-street while I took the bill - when I came out I told him that they knew nothing of any such parties; he said he knew not how that could be, because there had been a bill paid there; I think I left him then, but I saw him several times afterwards - I believe he frequently came to Mr. Hipkiss' inn. and he once or twice called on me; I said to him,"These bills stand in a very awkward state;" he said he would pay them this time and that time, but he never did.
JOHN WILLIAMS. I lived at No. 35, King-street, during the whole of 1828. I believe the prisoner's brother, whose name is John, lodged with me about fourteen or fifteen months, but not in 1828, it was in 1827; I never saw the prisoner himself there.(Bill read as in the indictment.)
Prisoner's Defence. The moment Mr. Hipkiss waited on me I went with him, and had I had the least suspicion of these bills being forged. it is not likely that I should have gone there; my reason for saying I would pay these bills, was, that I considered myself in some way responsible for the loss occasioned by my brother's conduct.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18290409-321]
Second London Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
998. RICHARD HARPER GREEN was indicted for that he, on the 17th of July, at St. Botolph Without, Aldersgate, had in his custody and possession a certain bill of exchange, which is as follows, that is to say:
£23: 14 Dudley, July 23,1828.
Two months after date, pay to my order, Twenty-three Pounds, Fourteen, Shillings, value received, for RICHARD FINCH. To Mr. James Lane, No. 4, Bull Head-court, King-street, Snow-hill, London.
on which said bill of exchange was then and there contained a certain false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance of the said bill of exchange, and which said false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance is as follows, "Accepted, James Lane;" and that he, the said Richard Harper Green, afterwards, to wit, on the same day, at the same parish, feloniously did utter and publish as true, the said false, forged, and counterfeited acceptance of the said bill of exchange, with intent to defraud Richard Finch, he, the said Richard Harper Green, at the time when he so uttered and published as true the said false, forget, and counterfeited acceptance, well knew the same to be false, forged. and counterfeited; against the Statute, & c.
RICHARD FINCH. I am a fender-maker, and live at Dudley, in Worcestershire - the prisoner sent me orders down into the country for goods. On the 17th of July he said he owed me 38l. 15s. 6d; he said he would give me this bill for 23l. 14s., and he would pay me the rest - he gave me the bill, and I took it with me into the country; he agreed to pay me the 15l. 1s. 6d., but he did not - at the time he gave me this bill I was at Mr. Stevens', the Rose and Crown public-house, in Little Britain; this is the bill - my son endorsed the bill in my presence; I paid it away, and it came back to me in the country.(The bill was here read as in the indictment.)
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. This bill became due on the 26th of September last? A. Yes; I had it returned to me unpaid - I staid about a fortnight, and then came to town - I went to Bull Head-court, and inquired about Mr. Lane; they said no such person lived there - I saw the prisoner afterwards, and he said he would give me another bill; I said it would be of no use - he did give me a bill for 18l.; this is it - it is drawn upon the prisoner; his brother was with him - (bill read)
£18: 0: 0 Dudley, October 28, 1828, Two months after date pay to my order, Eighteen Pounds, value received, for RICHARD FINCH.
To R.H.Green, No. 11, Harper-street, New Kent-road. Accepted, R.H.GREEN.
Q. Did not his brother promise to see this hill paid? A. Yes - I cannot say when I made this charge against the prisoner; it was about five or six weeks ago, since he was taken up - it was long after the second bill became due; I had been stopping at his house several times in the interval, and this last time I lived and slept there for nine or ten nights - I think not more; that was after the second bill was returned - he was taken on another bill before I gave charge of him; I have seen John Green since the prisoner was charged with this bill, but he had nothing to do with it; I had one bill before on Mr. Lane, which the prisoner sent down into the country - I paid it away, and it was paid; I sold John Green some fenders, and I took a bill of him, drawn on Nicholson, for them.
COURT. Q. Was there another bill, which became due in February? A. Yes; I was in the country when it became due - I saw John Green last about five weeks ago; I have since been there, and he is removed.
THOMAS BROOKS. I am a housekeeper, and live at No. 4, Bull's Head-court, Snow-hill. I have kept the house these three years; no person named Lane has lodged there during that time - I let two rooms, but to no person of that name.
JAMES JOHNSON. I live at the same house. I know no such name as Lane there; I know the prisoner, and he knew perfectly well where I lived; I have done little jobs for him now and then, when he has asked me, for the last two years - he has very often been to the house.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he enquiring after a person of the name of Lane? A. No; he was at the house about a week before he was taken, but I had no conversation with him on that occasion - I am not master of the house.
COURT. Q. Was he every there in the course of 1828? A. Yes, my Lord, I am certain of that; he has been there to send me on errands on such a thing as that - he lived in the New Kent-road.
JOHN SWAIN. I am an officer. I went to Bull's Headcourt, but could find no such person as Lane.
RICHARD FINCH. I took another bill on Lane from the prisoner - I paid that away, and have not seen it since.
Prisoner's Defence. This business was a transaction of my brother's, John Green; it was he that brought orders to me, and requested me to send the goods, saying Mr. Lane was a responsible person, and the bill would be paid; I had sent one lot of goods, for which he gave me a bill; that was paid; in consequence of that he came to me for other goods, and I had them sent - he gave me the bill which is now in question; it was entirely his transaction, and I depended on him: I never saw Lane myself, nor knew whether any such person lived there or not - he took the goods from me, and brought the bill to me accepted.
BARNARD BOYLE. I am clerk to a notary. I presented this bill for 15l. 6s. on James Lane, at No. 4, Bull's Head-court, Snow-hill; I do not recollect who gave the answer, but it was, "Pay to-morrow," and the money was paid the next day.
RICHARD FINCH re-examined. When he gave me the bill, it had the acceptance of James Lane on it; the reason the other bill was for only 18l. was, he asked me if I would have an 18l. bill, I said, Yes, as I wanted it to pay my brassman.
JURY to JAMES JOHNSON. Q. Have you any remembrance of his giving you any orders to pay a bill that might be presented to your house? A. No, he never mentioned such a thing - I gave an answer to this bill, that Mr. Green was not there; I did not say when it would be paid - the prisoner asked me if there was a bill presented, and I said, Yes, and gave him the memorandum, and he said,"Tell them I will call to-morrow."
COURT. Q. Did John Green ever give you any orders? A. No.
MR. BODKIN. Q. This conversation took place after the bill had been brought? A. Yes.
JURY. Q. Had you any previous notice that a bill would be brought? A. No; he came and asked me if there had not been a bill brought - I am quite sure there has not been a person of the name of Lane lodging there these four years, since I have been in the house; I was in the habit of taking him letters, and several bills came from the bankers, and I gave answer he was not there, or something of that kind.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 47.
Recommended to mercy; the Jury believing he intended to pay the bill; and was greatly influenced by his brother.
[trialAccount t18290409-322]
Fifth Middlesex Jury.
999. THOMAS GEORGE HAWKINS and JOHN BALLARD were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Smith Carr, on the 17th of March, at St. Andrews, Holborn, and stealing therein 1 chest, value 7s.; 1 tin-box, value 2s.; 50 sovereigns, 10 crowns, 6 half-crowns, 30 shillings, 4 sixpences, and three 20l. Bank-notes, his property, against the Statute; and GEORGE BALLARD and ANN (HIS WIFE) were indicted for feloniously receiving, on the same day, at the same parish, 22 sovereigns, part and parcel of the said monies, they well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute.
MR. SMITH conducted the prosecution.
MR. JOHN SMITH CARR. I am a shopkeer, and live at No. 90, Charles-street, Hatton-garden, in the parish of St. Andrew, Holborn. I occupy the two shops and two attics, in which I have two persons put in to take care of the house; it is a corner house - I let the house to different lodgers; I let one kitchen to a Mr. Ewer, the other kitchen I keep for the accommodation of the lodgers - the cellar may be about ten yards from the kitchen-door - I leave my shop every night for my residence at Holloway; I left it on Tuesday evening, the 17th of March, as near half-past nine o'clock as I can recollect - before I left it, I took down my cash-box into my cellar, in which was enclosed three 20l. notes, I can swear, but I believe there were four, upwards of 50 sovereigns, from 5l. to 7l. in silver, and amongst the silver was a bad crown-piece, with a piece cut out of the edge of it nearly the eighth of an inch, and two bills of exchange - I locked my cash-box, put it into an iron chest in the cellar; I locked the chest and the cellar-door - my shop was shutting up at this time; I waited till I saw the shop-doors closed, and then set off for Holloway - the next morning I received some information, a little after nine o'clock, and went down into the cellar with a light; I found the cellar-door broken open, and the iron chest gone, and a chisel was lying on the ledge where the iron-chest had been placed - the chisel is in the possession of the constable; I should know it again- I found my iron-chest the same morning at the house of a chimney-sweeper, named Brown, in Feather's-court, Fox-court - I have no doubt it was my chest; the prisoner Hawkins came to live with me, I think in December, 1826, and left me last September; he must have seen me going down stairs to deposit my cash-box in the cellar, as as it has been my custom for the last six months - the kitchen is at the corner of Charles-street.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You sleep at Holloway? A. Yes. Mr. Ewer, Miss Wellyer and some other persons lodge at my house in Charles-street, some of them had servants; one of their servants is here- I always sleep at Holloway; it is there my family reside.
JAMES OWEN. I am servant to Mr. Ewer. He lodges at Mr. Carr's; on the night of the 17th of March, I left the kitchen window shut down but not fastened, and one shutter shut - I cannot tell what time of night it was; I have been there a year and a quarter - and that was the usual mode of leaving the window and shutter; I went down the next morning, and saw the kitchen door open which had been fastened with a padlock; the padlock was hanging by the hasp, but the hasp had been forced out- the window sash was up, and the shutters both back; this was about nine o'clock - I did not observe the cellar door then, but I did afterwards, and saw a piece had been broken out of it; I did not go into the cellar.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How many servants were in the house altogether at that time? A. Four- some men and some women; I left the house at nine o'clock at night; I cannot say that I left the other servants all up - I suppose they would not be gone to bed; they are not here; I do not know whether they were up or not.
ALEXANDER BOWERS. I am a pot boy to Mr. Bowers, who keeps the Pewter Platter public-house, in Charles-street - I am his nephew. On the night of the 17th of March I was in Charles-street about half-past nine o'clock, near the house of Mr. Carr - I saw a person near his house, it was a young man - I was two or three yards from him, and I stopped and looked at him; he called me several had words, and said, if I did not go on he would kick my little guts out - that was John Ballard; I went on, and stood in the middle of the street, and saw a young man come out of Mr.Carr's area; John Ballard was at that time standing against the gateway; they crossed the road together - I have no doubt about John Ballard being the person - I had known him by sight before, and observed him particularly.
COURT. Q. Did you observe the dress of the other man? A. Yes - he had a dark blue coat and trousers.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How old are you? A. Eleven years: I have never been in a church or a chapel in my life; I have never been taught my prayers; I know what the meaning of an oath is - if I tell a lie I shall go to hell; no one told me to say that - I have heard it from several people just before I went to the office - it was not told me by any of the officers - it was told me by a gentleman, who was a stranger to me -I did not ask him what business he had to tell me that- I have never seen him since, and had no conversation with him but that; he came into our house about seven o'clock in the morning, while I was clearing up the place - it was the day I was coming up to Hatton-garden - he asked me, whether I knew the nature of an oath? -I told him no, I did not: he said, if you tell a lie, you will go to hell; he stopped in the tap-room - he did not say, if the magistrate asked me, I was to give him that answer - the gentleman had never spoken to me in my life before - I should not know him again; no one ever told me the nature of an oath but that gentleman; I have a father living, he never told me so.
MR. SMITH. Q. Your uncle keeps a public-house, and has a good deal to do? A. yes; we work on Sundays as as well as other days.
COURT. Q. Was that the first day you ever heard hell mentioned? A. No; I suppose bell to be a wicked place - that person told me if I told lies I should go to that wicked place - I believe that now; I have heard about religion before that - I do not know the Commandments -I do not know my duty to God - I have lived in that public-house three years; I have known Hawkins, as living in the service of Mr. Carr - if the other young man had been Hawkins I should have known him; but I am sure it was not; when they crossed the road they went towards Brooke-street.
SAMUEL SANDELL. I am a hair-dresser. and live at No.1, Dorrington-street, Brook's-market - I occupy the shop and parlour; there is a yard just large enough for the water-hutt, and there is a tenement there, consisting of two rooms, which is occupied by George Ballard and his wife; they have three sons, who live with them - a passage leads into their apartment; I have a door leading from my back parlour into the passage. On Tuesday night, the 17th of March, a little after ten o'clock, it might be a quarter past, I heard something fall down in the passage very heavy - it gave an alarm; I took the candle, and went out - I saw two persons; John Ballard was one - I have no doubt of him; he was sitting on a chest, and the other was standing on the step of the door with his back towards me; I said, "What is all this noise about?" Ballard told me to call his mother, and said he wanted no light - the noise I heard shook the partition: I asked Ballard who his mother was, and he said Ballard - I opened the back door, called Mrs. Ballard, and then I went into my room; the next morning I opened my door to get some water, and Mrs. Ballard said to me, "Come up stairs, and I will shew you the box that was let down last night" - she said it was let down by her son; I went up stairs, and saw a wooden box with some mouldings in it for cielings, such as plasterers use - I did not go near enough to see whether the mouldings were broken, or not; I cannot say whether it was the box which I had seen the night before - I did not examine it at all.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You would not know what sort of a noise it would make? A. No; it is not a private passage - there are a good many persons come into it: I knew John Ballard before, but I did not know him that night till he spoke, and ordered me to call his mother; I believe they have three sons and one daughter, but John Ballard has a more manly voice than the others - the eldest of the others is about fourteen; I do not swear to him by his voice - I am not very found of dogs, but I have had a good deal to do with them.
Cross-examined by MR. BONKIN. Q. There was another person? A. Yes, on the stop of the door - his back was towards me; my candle was lighted, and I went out directly after I heard the noise - I had not heard any person call a man at the door to help him.
COURT. Q. You do not know that it was Ballard, except by his calling his mother? A. No - I saw his face then; I did not know him till he spoke; I have seen him before going up and down - I did not see his face; he held his head down - I took no notice that night of the face or person of the other man; his mother came down directly.
ANDREW DOWDEN. I am a cabinet-maker. On the night of Tuesday, the 17th of March, I was with the last witness, about ten o'clock; I heard a great noise in the passage - it seemed as if all the partition was coming down; what it was I cannot tell - I went into the passage along with Sandell; I saw two young men - one was sitting on the box, and the other standing on the step of the door; I was not near the box- I was behind Mr. Sandell: I was not near enough to see what sort of a box it was - the one on the box said to Mr.Sandell, with an oath, "Go in, for I want no light here;" he asked him to call his mother, and Sandell asked him what his mother's name was - he said Mrs. Ballard; Sandell called her and when she came down she asked him for a light; he did not give her one, as the young man said he wanted no light there; I did not notice who they were.
JAMES OSHORNE. My father is a coach-wheelwright; we live in Dorrington-street, Brookes-market - I was twelve years old on the 24th of March; I go to church-I know the Lord's Prayer; I do not know what the nature of an oath is; I know I must always speak the truth, if I do not, I shall go to hell.
COURT. Q. What do you call the Lord's Prayer? how does it begin? A."Our Father, which art in Heaven," &c.
MR. SMITH. Q. Do you remember, on the evening of the 17th of March, going on an errand? A. Yes, my mother sent me about half-past ten o'clock at night: I had to pass through the passage of the house we live in; there is a partition between Mr. Sandell's parlour and the passage - I saw a chest in the passage, and John Ballard standing by it; I know him-that is him; I have no doubt about it - I am sure it was him; there was another person standing on the step of the door - that person was George Hawkins;that is him: I sawMrs.Ballardcome down - John Ballard told her she was always a-bed; she had no light-she asked for one from Mr. Sandell; John Ballard said, "We want no light here" - I saw John Ballard and Hawkings take hold of each end of the chest and take it up stairs; it appeared to me to be a heavy chest: before I came down, while I was up stairs, I heard something fall very heavy in the passage - I went on my errand, and returned in about five minutes; when I re turned I heard a very heavy knocking in Ballard's room- I was in my own room, which is about three feet from Ballard's house; the noise appeared to be like something very hard - like something knocking against iron;Ihave been near a blacksmith's shop, and heard the noise directly I came back; I went to bed when the noise had ceased,
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you examined at the Police Office? A. Yes; I stated there that Mr. Sandell came to the passage, and the light was asked for.
Cross-examined by Mr. BODKIN. Q. Do you live in small house? A. No, they live in a small house in the yard; I knew Ballards before -I had never seen Hawkins before; Mr.Sandell staid with the light till they were up stairs - that was two or three minutes, or more than that; it might be five minutes he said at his own parlour door in the passage; and there is a passage, and then there is another passage goes down; and his door is in that - Hawkins was at the step of the door at the end of the passage next the street, a good way from the candle.
COURT. Q. How could you see him so well? A. I saw him when he came along the passage with the chest- when I came back they were up stairs.
ANN OSBORNE. I am the mother of this witness. On Tuesday night, the 17th of March, I sent him out on an errand; I heard a very heavy fall in the passage before he went down, and I kept him a minute or two till I heard the sound of voices, and then I sent him down; this was a little after ten o'clock - after he came back I asked him what that swearing was, which I heard in the passage, and in a few minutes I heard something like a very heavy breaking; I cannot exactly describe it - it was very heavy,like knocking against something very hard, very loud, and it shook my room; it continned about twenty minutes, more or less, and appeared to come from the back house, which is out in the yard, and which is occupied by the Ballards - there was no other house from which it could come.
MARY DENTRY. I am a grand-daughter of Mr. Sandell, and live there. On the night of the 17th of March I saw two men about twenty minutes after eleven o'clock, just at the corner of Dorrington-street; I do not know who they were - they were going from the yard, up towards Brook-street; one of them had something on his shoulder, which appeared very heavy by the way he carried it: When I saw them they were near our door,about six feet from the passage.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You cannot tell whether they had been in the yard? A. No.
JAMES HANKS. I am a carver and gilder, and live at No. I, Dorrington-street, Brook's-market. On Wednesday, the 18th of March, about seven o'clock in the evening, I was at the street door in company with Culmore, and heard the cry of Murder! which seemed to come from the back premises occupied by Ballards; I know old Ballard and his wife well - Culmore and I went to the back premises, and when we got into the yard I heard the voice of Mrs. Ballard, which proceeded from Ballard's apartments; she said Joseph Ince should not have the money; I heard a kind of scuffle and other voices in the room - I heard Joseph Ince say if any person was mistrustful of his having the money he would give it up to any one they chuse; we then heard three or four voices say, "Let old Ballard have it;" we then heard the counting of money, which appeared as if counted on a table or a board; and it appeared, by the sound, to be sovereigns - we heard luch count one, two three,till he came to twenty-one sovereigns; and then there were two half-sovereigns, which he said made 22l. - we then heard a young woman's voice say she did not expect her brother John to wear two faces under one hat that morning; when the voices said Mr.Ballard should have it, Ballard said he would take care of it, it should not see either sun or moon.
COURT. Q. Are you sure it was old Ballard's voice said that? A. Yes, it was then nearly eight o'clock,
Cross-examined by MR.PHILLIPS. Q. These people must have taken uncommon pains for you to hear them; did they talk very loud? A. They did talk loudish; the upper door was closed - I know the sound of sovereigns from shillings if they chinked, that I will swear; I was at the bottom of the stairs; there are ten stairs - I was about three yards from them; I have always gone by the name of James Hanks, unless they call me Jem; Hanks is the surname I have always gone by, never by any other; I was never here before, nor in the other Court - there was a charge made against me at Hatton-garden, by Mr. Dupree, of No. 10,Dorrington-street. a composition maker, for taking a sixpence and 3d. in halfpence; I am sure there was never any other charge made against me; there was no charge about jewellery;I was tried at Hicks-hall upon Mr. Dupree's charge, and acquitted - it is seven years ago; I am now nineteen years of age; Mr. Dupree was my master then - I live up stairs in the front house at No. 1, Dorrington-street; there might be twelve or fourteen people living in the front house or more; the back house is not more than four feet from the front house - I cannot say whether the people in the front house could hear this; they might be out.
MR. SMITH. Q. Have you any doubt of your being correct in what you have stated? A. What I have stated I did hear.
CHARLES CULMORE. I am a cutler, and live in Beanchamp street. I was in company with Hanks on Wednesday night,the 18th of March, about half-past six or nearer seven o'clock - we were standing at the street door, No.1, Dorrington-street, and I heard Murder called; we went into the back yard near the premises,occupied by the Ballards only - I heard Mrs. Ballard's voice, she was beating Ivy Ballard, and somebody said, "Don't kick him, Mrs. Ballard; and I heard Ballard's sister say,"Oh, my poor brother! I did not think he carried two faces under one hat this morning;" I then heard Mrs.Ballard say she would have the money. she wanted the money - and two or three voices said,"The old man shall have it."
Cross-exmined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it before the money was counted that the sister said she did not think her brother carried two faces under one hat? A. Yes; I was standing at the yard door - lvy then came down with a light, as he had some suspicion of somebody overhearing them; Hanks went into the passage to avoid being seen, and I went into the water-closet; and said in a good loud voice, "Who is there?" I said, "Me;" he said who was me - and I did not answer him; Hanks was somewhere about the passage - I do not know whether he was near enough to hear Ivy call; Ivy looked about, and opened the yard door, but could not see Hanks - he looked about the yard and the dust-hole, and then went up stairs; I believe Hanks did not come back into the yard.
COURT. Q. You heard nothing but conversation? A. No; I did not hear the money counted out, nor any thing about that, because I was further off than Hanks was - I was by the yard door,holding it open in case any body should come with a light.
Q. What do you mean by saying you did not hear the money counted out? A. Because Hanks heard it - he said so to me.
Q. You told the Magistrate they counted money - how came you to state that, if you did not hear it? A. Because old Ballard said it should not see sun nor moon; and so I thought that he did have money.
Q. Was that your reason for supposing he did have money, and for saying they counted it? A. Yes, I have got a slight memory - I did not recollect it.
JOSEPH MARRIAGE. I live with James Brown, a chimney-sweeper, in Feathers-court, Fox-court, between Brooke-street and Gray's Inn-lane. I found an iron-chest on some old ruins, about six o'clock on Wednesday morning, the 18th of March - it was where a house had been pulled down; I dragged it into my master's yard - when my master came he asked where I got it - I told him, and he gave information.
COURT. Q. Was it left in your master's yard? A. Yes, and Mr. Carr came to look at it about an hour and a half or two hours afterwards - it is here.
JOHN LEWIS. I am a bell-hanger and locksmith, and live at No.13, Tothill - street, Laystall-street. I remember being at the Blackamoor's liead public-house nearly all day on the 17th of March - I remember seeing Hawkins and John Ballard there in the afternoon; I knew them perfectly-these are the persons; when I went in, Ballard was playing at bagatelle - there was young man there whom I knew, named Williams; Ballard lost two pots of ale - he paid the landlord for three pints, and said he would pay the other next day; the landlord said he ought not to play without he had money - he then went away; Hawkins then played for half a pound of beaf steaks, and lost - he went out to fetch them, but did not return; I went home to tea about five o'clock, and re turned about seven - Hawkins came in and sat down by the side of Hawkins - they spoke to each other, but I did not hear what they said; Ballard rose up, pulled a chisel out of his breast, and showed it to Hawkins - Hawkins turned round side ways, and said, "It is just the thing;" my attention was drawn to it; I thought there was something going on wrong, and I observed it particularly, so that if I saw it again I should know it - I have seen it since in the possession of the beadle; I did not take hold of it till I was at Hatton-garden - I was sure it was the same that I had seen: they said about an hour longer at the public-house, and then went away together - this was about a quarter past eight, I believe, but the clock did not go right; they returned about a quarter past ten, and called out a young man named Riley, who had been asleep there for four or five hours they touched him, and he followed them out; they paid something - I remained there till a quarter before twelve; I am quite sure this is the chisel- it is made out of an old file.
Cross-examined by MR.PHILLIPS. Q. This chisel they were showing openly? A. No.; I had an opportunity of seeing it for about a couple of seconds - I knew it instantly when I saw it the next day; there were some of Ballard's relations in the public-house, playing at bagatelle, and one young man that beat him out of the steaks- I have been here before for my own property; I was tried for a game cock, as they called,it, but I was honourably acquitted - I was not up above a minute and a half, and you were my counsel; there were one or two witnesses examined, but it did not last above a minute and a half - I never heard of any body saying they could swear a spot off a tea-kettle; I never said that unless I got a pound, I would swear a spout off a tea-kettle - I never said, in the presence of Badland and another person, that I knew nothing about this robbery; they offered me 10l., but I did not see why I should not be in it as well as other people; they tried to buy me to keep out of the way - I was never accused of any thing beside the game cock, unless it was fighting now and then; there never was a young woman who lost any clothes, and brought a charge against me - it was in October Session I was tried about the game cuck; I know Badland perfectly well - he is the landlord of that public-house, and I know Richard Badland - it is William Badland who keeps the public-house; I do not know John Griffths by name-I never told any body I made this chisel myself, but I told where a piece of a silk roller came from that was to it, and Crowder, Mr. Ballard's brother, had some to sell.
WILLIAM BADLAND. I keep the Blackamoor's Head in Brookes-market; I have kept it about three months, On Tuesday afternoon. the 17th March, I recollect seeing Hawkins and Ballard the younger in my house; I knowthem - these are them; there were several other persons there - in consequence of their having played a games, Ballard came and paid me for one pint of ale, and left one pint unpaid; they were in and out, in the course of the afternoon - they went out, and returned about eight o'clock, and were there from ten till half-past ten; on their return Hawkins asked me what he owed me - I told him, and he paid me 4s. 9d. or 4s.9 1/2d., with five shillings in silver, and I saw from seven to ten shillings in his hand; I saw Lewis there in the afternoon, and in the evening also - he was three from three to four hours altogether.
Cross-examined by MR.BODKIN. Q. Did you know of any gambling going forward in your house? A. Yes, bagatelle; I did not state before the Magistrate there was no gambling: in my house that night - I said ther were no cards; I will swear I told the Magistrate I did not see any gambling: I am not in the habit of frequenting that room- I was told of it by my servant, the day following; I never said" You should not have paid for what you can't pay for," to the best of my knowledge: I did not know there had been any gambling till the next day - I do not believe I said that.
MR. SMITH. Q. When he came to the bar, and paid you for one pint, and left one pint unpaid, what did you say? A. I said I was not in the habit of giving credit, and I would not, and he said he would return and pay me.
JOHN COMPTON. I keep the Three Jolly Butchers public-house, in Brookes-market. On Tuesday, the 17th of March, about half-past eleven o'clock at night, Hawkings and John Ballard came to my house; I have no doubt they are the persons - I knew Hawkins before; he said he had come to pay me what he owed me, as he had been up to the neighbourhood of Clare-market, and had won 25s. at cards; he gave me a crown-piece, which was not a good one, and I told him it would not do - he said to Ballard, "John, you must lend me 5s.;" Ballard put down two half - crowns, and a penny, and I took it up - they then went into the tap-room, and had half a gallon of ale.
THOMAS JEFFREYS. I keep the George public - house, in Brooke-street. On the 17th of March, about a quarter before twelve o'clock at night, Hawkins, John Ballard, and another person, whose name I have heard is Riley, came into my house; they called for three glasses of wine, and one of them, which I believe was Hawkins, put down a crown-piece, which I refused, as I was convinced it was not a good one, and they gave me a shilling - the crown-piece had rather a deep cut on the edge, rather wider than a piece would be cut, to ascertain whether it was good or bed.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. You will not swear who it was? A. I think it was Hawkins; I have a good deal of bad money offered me - some people do cut the edge of money, to ascertain whether it is good, but that. is not my way; this was very bad - it was white metal; I looked at it for some time because I happened to have one before.
MR. SMITH. Q. Did it appear to be cut for ascertaining whether it was good or bad? A. Yes, but more cut than is usual for that purpose.
WILLIAM JOSCELYNE. I am a butcher, and live in Brooke-street, Holborn. At Seven o'clock in the morning, on Wednesday, the 18th of March, I saw Haswkins, John Ballard, and Riley come from under a gateway; there is a grating opposite my master's shop, leading to the common sewer - I saw Hawkins throw something down the grating, it appeared to me to be a crown-piece; they went on: I told my master, and we went to look, but could not find it.
Cross-examined by MR.BODKIN. Q. You thought there was something to be got? A. No, the grating was taken up, but we found no crown-piece.
COURT. Q. Do you think he did throw any thing down? A. yes, and I think it went into the sewer.
GEORGE BEAN. I am a tripeman, and live in Beauchamp-street. Brookes-market. On Wednesday, the 18th of March, I saw Hawkins and Riley in West-street, Smithfield; there were several young men with them, and they were quarrelling - I saw Hawkins put his hand into his pocket, and pull out some sovereings: he said to another young mans that he would fight him - I think here were ten or twelve sovereigns; I cannot exactly say how many there were - Hawkins was not so drunk as Riley; Riley was very drunk.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you been much in Hawkins's company? A. No, never.
WILLIAM WINGFIELD HAWKINS. I am ten years of age; I go to church, and say my prayers: if I tell lies I shall go to everlasting torments; I have taken an oath to tell the truth. I remember my brother, Thomas George Hawkins, coming home to my mother's house one Wednesday - he said he had won 5l. at cards the night before; he sent me to fetch his clothes from the pawnbroker's, and gave me 6l.1s.
JOHN HOARE. I am pot- boy at the Crown public-house, in West Smithfield. I remember Hawkins and Riley coming to that house, but I cannot tell the day - I think it was in March; Hawkins paid me and my master's niece for three pints of ale, and one bottle of port wine; I saw some sovereigns and some silver in his hand- he then went out; Riley and another young man fought; I saw Hawkins pull out a sovereign, and ask who had a sovereign to lay on the fight.
JOHN BARNLEY. I am a constable. On the 18th of March, in the forenoon, I apprehended John Ballard, in company with Riley, who was very drunk: I took them both, but Riley was discharged; I searched John Ballard in the lock-up yard at Hatton-garden - I found in his waistcoat pocket 2s., and in his right shoe five sovereigns; I told him I apprehended him on suspicion of a robbery - he said he had found the sovereigns down Bagniggewells-road that morning, as he was taking a walk, and he knew nothing of the robbery; I took Hawkins about an hour afterwards, at the Globe public-house, in Hatton-garden, at the corner of Charles-street - he was very much in liquor, and attempted to pay for a glass of liquor with a sovereign, which I took from him, and in his pocket I found 11s. in silver; I took George Ballard at his won house, on the Friday following - I took the mother in Holborn some short time afterwards, but I found nothing on them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You searched the place, I suppose? A. Yes; I found no bills of exchange, or any thing of that sort; I produce the chisel.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Was not this the day after the robbery? A. Yes, about twelve o'clock; the Globe is near Hatton-garden office; I found nothing on him but a sovereign and 11s.
MR. CARR. This is the chisel I found on the ledge where the chest had been standing.
JOHN LEWIS. This is the chisel I saw in the publichouse, in the possession of Hawkins and John Ballard.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you ever tell any body that you made this chisel? A. No, I never said I made the jemmy; I never said so to this young man ( John Griffiths) - I have had conversation with him many times; I never said there was a jemmy produced, and that I knew more about the transaction than any one else - he never said to me,"You made this jemmy," nor did I answer I did - I have knwon him before; he is a particular friend of mine.
Cross-examined by MR.BODKIN. Q. You said you saw the chisel for two seconds? A. Yes; Ballard shewed it to Hawkins, and Hawkins laid hold of it in his hand; I do not think I said that before.
JOHN BARNLEY re-examined. Q. Have you tried this chisel any where? A. Yes, to the kitchen door at Mr. Carr's, and it exactly fitted the wrench in the door where the lock was forced: I also tried it to the inner cellar door, and it corresponded there.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you say that at Hatton - garden? A. No.
ANDREW LLOYD. I am an officer of Hatton-garden. I produce an iron chest which I got from a man of the name of Brown, in Feathers-court, Fox-court - I have had it ever since; I searched Ballard's house, but found nothing; I saw a deal box there, which contained what I believe they call enrichments - it was not broken at all they were plaster of Paris chimney ornaments - if that box had been let fall. they would have been broken - if I had let one of them fall it would have been broken.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you broght it here? A. No, it was not very heavy; I searched the the house accurately on the 18th, and found on 20l. Bank notes or bills - it was about one o'clock in the day -Barnley and I were together.
MR. SMITH. Q. Bills of exchange and notes are more traceable than gold and silver, are they not? A. Yes; I have a blue coat here, which Ballard's mother told me I had just been taken off that morning.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. You cannot tell how long it had been taken off? A. No, I was at the office when that coat was shown to Bowers.
ALEXANDER BOWERS. That is not the coat one of the men had on, the night in question.
COURT. Q. How do you know? A. It looked a newer one - it had shiny buttons.
MR. CARR. This is my chest.
JOSEPH MARRIAGE. This is the chest I saw, and I dragged it into the yard.
JAMES OSBORNE. That is like the chest I saw carried out - I could not tell whether it was wood or iron.
HAWKIN's Defence. I solemnly declare my innocence of the crime, and I hope the Jury will take all the statements into consideration in my behalf.
JOHN BALLARD 's Defence. I have witnesses to prove that I was not in Charles-street that night; and that I was not in the public-house at the time Lewis swears I gave the chisel out of my hosom; he said it was about nine o'clock, and now he says after ten.
GEORGE BALLARD 's Defence. I never saw that chest, nor any thing belonging to it in my life; I never had such property as that young man swears to, for these sixteen years, except it was my employer's.
ANN BALLARD 's Defence. Hanks says he will swear to my voice, but he never knew me till within five weeks; we had not given up housekeeping more than five weeks before this happened: I will be on my solemn oath that he never saw me on that night, or heard me, except going out for my supper beer, or so. I hope you will not suffer, the evidence of such a boy as that to convict me; the first at Hatton-garden, and I was not afraid of going; he knows his mother went down on her knees to the gentleman, and he being a humane gentleman, would not take advantage of her son.
JOHN GRIFFITHS. I am a draper. I have no knowledge of the prisoners; I believe I never spoke to them in my life: Lewis had been giving evidence, and he told me a jemmy had been produced at the office; I asked if he knew where it came from; he said he knew all about it; I asked if he knew where it came from; he said he did; I said, "I suppose you made it?" Yes (said he) I did."
MR. SMITH. Q. Who do you live with? A. I am out of a situation; the last situation I had was with John Edward Smee, Crown-court, Cheapside - I left on the 1st of March, and I have not been employed since then; I have been occasionally at the Blackamoor's Head, not very often, perhaps twice or three times a week; I would not swear I have not been there four or five times, I should not think I have been more than three times - I have played at bagatelle there perhaps half a dozen times; between the 1st and 17th of March I do not think I played more than three or four times; I have played at cards perhaps three or four times, at whist; that is the only game I recollect - I do not know the game of put, therefore I do not think that I ever played at it - I do not recollect playing at put, or any game except whist; I have made one at a game of put, but I do not know the game; I have not played at put in that house, to my recollection, and I will swear I did not play with any of the prisoners at the bar - I may have played at put, but not with one of them; I will swear I have not played any game with any of them: I have seen Hawkins there, perhaps, half a dozen times, but I do not recollect having ever changed a word with either of them - I went to Hatton-garden with Mr. Badland; I know him perfectly well- I have been an inmate of his house for the last three weeks; I went from curiosity more than any thing else, as I had heard of the robbery - I know Lewis by sight, nothing more; I was not inquisitive about this chisel, but he put a number of questions to me; he came first and sat down very near me, at Mr. Badland's, and commenced the conversation; there was several persons present, but I do not recollect any one that I knew - it did not occur to me to make any inquiries, or to communicate this conversation; it was no business of mine - it was on the day of the second or third examination.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How came you to leave the drapers? A. They failed and were in the Gazette; I had been with them about two months. I am single.
COURT. Q. What did Lewis say? A. He turned towards me, and said there was a jemmy produced at the office; that was what began it.
HANNAH CROWDER. I know John Ballard; his mother is my husband's sister. On the evening of the 17th of March I saw him at my house, I think about nine o'clock; he staid, I suppose, nearly three quarters of an hour, and left near ten - I live at No. 17, Bell-court, Gray's Inn-lane; he worked for me some years - he had home a good character; I have intrusted him to pay and receive many pounds, and never had a furthing mistake with him.
CHARLES RINGER. I am a locksmith, and live in Bellcourt. I have known John Ballard eighteen years. I never heard any thing against his character in my life; he has always been a good young man.
Cross-examined by MR. SMITH. Q. You know the other Ballards? A. Yes, by sight; I have not met the woman any where within the last six weeks; I have never since the robbery said that either that woman or her daughter had the sovereigns about her; I have never said that either the mother or the daughter had the sovereigns concealed in their hair; I never said that to
Mr. Carr.
MR. CARR re-examined. Q. Does the cellar open into the kitchen? A. No, into the passage, about ten yards, from the kitchen door; there is no opening from the cellar to the street - you go into the cellor without going into the air.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Were the servants that were in the house belonging to yourself? A. They are not servants of mine - they were put in by myself to take care of the house; I give them the place to sleep in - I never sleep there my self-they are not servants of mine,neither of them.
Three witnesses gave Hawkins a good character, one deposed the same for John Ballard, and four for George Ballard.
HAWKINS - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 20.
JOHN BALLARD - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 18.
GEORGE BALLARD - GUILTY. Aged 52.
ANN BALLARD - GUILTY Aged 40.
George and Ann Ballard recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Confined Six Months.
